## Reviewer accuracy register

Accuracy register for the source reviewer. The reviewer is the curriculum foundation, not an authority: items here are flagged where it is outdated, contradicted by a current source, missing context, or needs verification. 'verified-issue' means the problem was confirmed against the cited external source; 'needs-verification' means a likely issue that has not yet been confirmed. A full line-by-line review of all 79 chapters has NOT been done — only targeted checks (chapters 1 [CLIA, prions], 26, 35, 40, Appendix B, and an automated scan).

Automated scan: Automated scan (2026-10-07): 23 High-Yield/Number-Vault/Trap items cite a dated guideline, edition, or approval year and need a currency check; 134 High-Yield Hits use only/always/never wording and should be checked for overstatement when their lessons are built. The reviewer's own release record lists 22 items flagged for human review and a 34-item human-review queue that were not included in the EPUB.

- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 1 · Exam Traps #2 — Lists 'New York, D.C.' as CLIA-exempt alongside deemed accreditors. CMS's List of Exempt States names Washington (full exemption) and New York (partial exemption); D.C. is not listed. The trap's dodge also opens with 'Right', so the Trap/Dodge format is inverted here. _(evidence: [CMS. List of Exempt States under CLIA, v. 08/15/2025](https://www.cms.gov/files/document/list-exempt-states-august-2025.pdf))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #4 — States that breath tests, noninvasive bilirubin and workplace drug tests are NOT regulated by CLIA. 42 CFR 493.3 excludes forensic-only testing, research not reporting patient results, and SAMHSA-certified drug testing; it does not list breath tests. Whether a given breath or noninvasive test falls under CLIA needs verification (e.g. against FDA/CMS categorization). _(evidence: [42 CFR 493.3 Applicability (eCFR)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-493))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Gives prion surface decontamination as ≥5,000 ppm hypochlorite (≈1:5–1:10 household bleach) or 1N NaOH (same in Exam Simulator key Q1). WHO (1999, Annex III) and CDC (reviewed 2026) give 2N NaOH or undiluted sodium hypochlorite for 1 hour for surfaces and heat-sensitive instruments; 20,000 ppm is the instrument-soak strength. The reviewer also says glutaraldehyde does 'not work', whereas WHO lists it as variably/partially effective (practical message unchanged). _(evidence: [WHO Infection Control Guidelines for TSEs, WHO/CDS/CSR/APH/2000.3 (1999), Table 8 and Annex III](https://szu.gov.cz/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/WHO_CDS_CSR_APH_2000.3.pdf); [CDC. Infection Control for CJD (reviewed 2026-01-21)](https://www.cdc.gov/creutzfeldt-jakob/hcp/infection-control/index.html))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #13 — States 'eyewash within 100 ft' without the time criterion. OSHA 29 CFR 1910.151(c) requires suitable facilities 'within the work area for immediate emergency use' and sets no distance. A 1992 OSHA interpretation letter (construction) says equipment should be no more than 100 feet from the work station AND reachable within 10 seconds; later OSHA letters treat ANSI Z358.1 as non-enforceable guidance. The current ANSI/ISEA Z358.1 edition was not consulted. _(evidence: [29 CFR 1910.151(c) (eCFR)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-29/section-1910.151); [OSHA standard interpretation, July 20, 1992: Eyewash and shower equipment in construction](https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/standardinterpretations/1992-07-20-5); [OSHA standard interpretation, Nov 1, 2002: Additional clarification of using ANSI Z358.1 as guidance to comply with 1910.151(c)](https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/standardinterpretations/2002-11-01))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Presents HazCom/GHS as the 2012 rule only; OSHA amended the standard again on May 20, 2024 (89 FR 44356). The SDS threshold wording ('>1%, carcinogens >0.1%') simplifies Appendix A mixture cut-off values: ≥1.0% for many health hazards and ≥0.1% for carcinogens (and Category 1 germ-cell mutagens). The SDS-in-English requirement is correct (1910.1200(g)(2)). _(evidence: [29 CFR 1910.1200 (eCFR, up to date as of 2026-10-05): (g)(2); Appendix A Tables A.5.1, A.6.1, A.8.3](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-29/section-1910.1200))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 1 · Exam Traps #6 — Dates the bloodborne-pathogens standard to 1992; it was published in the Federal Register on December 6, 1991 (56 FR 64175). Also, OSHA's rule still requires 'universal precautions', while the reviewer says Standard Precautions replaced them (CDC terminology). _(evidence: [29 CFR 1910.1030 (eCFR) source note](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-29/section-1910.1030))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 40 · Number Vault #1 — States 35 ISBT blood group systems (>700 antigens). ISBT now registers 48 systems and 371 antigens (2025–2026 RCIBGT working-party report). _(evidence: [ISBT Red Cell Immunogenetics and Blood Group Terminology Working Party, Annual Report 2025–2026](https://isbtweb.foleon.com/annual-report/annual-report-2025/isbt-rci-working-party))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 40 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Bibliography and Ch. 74 Hit 2 cite AABB Standards (2019). The current edition is the 35th, effective April 1, 2026 (two-year cycle). AABB-based statements (two ABO determinations, sample validity, parentage PI threshold) need re-checking against the current edition. _(evidence: [AABB — Standards for Blood Banks and Transfusion Services (35th edition page)](https://aabb.org/standards-accreditation/standards/blood-banks-and-transfusion-services))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits — Bibliography cites CLSI M100 '2020 edition per chapter notes'. M100 is revised annually; the current edition is M100-Ed36 (January 26, 2026). Any breakpoint values in Ch. 58 may be outdated. _(evidence: [CLSI M100 — Performance Standards for Antimicrobial Susceptibility Testing, 36th ed. (product page)](https://clsi.org/shop/standards/M100))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Cites WHO 2010 semen values (volume 2–5 mL, concentration 15–50 million/mL, total motility ≥40%, progressive ≥32%). The WHO manual 6th edition (2021) revised the lower 5th-centile limits (volume 1.4 mL, concentration 16 million/mL, total motility 42%, progressive 30%, normal forms 4%) and states the 5th centiles alone do not diagnose infertility. The reviewer's '2–5 mL' also does not match WHO 2010's lower limit (1.5 mL). Same issue in the Ch. 26 Number Vault. _(evidence: [Boitrelle F, et al. The Sixth Edition of the WHO Manual for Human Semen Analysis: A Critical Review and SWOT Analysis. Life (Basel). 2021;11(12):1368. doi:10.3390/life11121368](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC8706130))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 35 · Number Vault #6 — Gives the acute-leukemia blast threshold as ≥20% per WHO 2017. WHO-HAEM5 (2022) removed the 20% requirement for most AML with defining genetic abnormalities (exceptions such as BCR::ABL1 and CEBPA), and the 2022 ICC uses 10% for AML with defining genetic abnormalities. The threshold is classification-dependent. _(evidence: [Haferlach T, et al. Overview on WHO-HAEM5 and the diagnostic relevance of genetic alterations for the classification. Medizinische Genetik. 2024;36:3. doi:10.1515/medgen-2024-2008](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC11006249))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 40 · Number Vault #2 — States '>62' Rh antigens. Not confirmed; the ISBT RH system antigen count should be checked against the current ISBT table.
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 31 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Core concept states MCHC is increased 'only' in spherocytosis. Dehydrated hereditary stomatocytosis (xerocytosis) also raises MCHC (Zama 2020); cold agglutinins raise it spuriously (Falvella 2021, La Gioia 2018), as can high bilirubin (Wu 2021); Falvella also links antiretroviral therapy and severe electrolyte disorders with increased MCHC. Wu 2021 found MCHC > 35.5 g/dL detected only 41% of hereditary spherocytosis cases. _(evidence: [Zama D, et al. Ital J Pediatr. 2020;46:102 (abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32703298/); [Falvella FS, et al. Scand J Clin Lab Invest. 2021;81(7):598-600 (abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34346820/); [La Gioia A, et al. J Clin Pathol. 2018;71(8):729-734 (abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29535214/); [Wu Y, Liao L, Lin F. J Clin Lab Anal. 2021;35(12):e24034](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8649336/))_
- **needs-verification** · outdated · Ch. 73 · Exam Traps #5 — Refers to '4 FDA-approved AD drugs'. Newer anti-amyloid antibodies have been approved since the older count; verify against current FDA approvals.

- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Appendix A — States ASCP weights of 17–22% for the four major areas. One secondary source gave 17–20% for Microbiology. The official ASCP guideline could not be retrieved to confirm.
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits — Regulatory content is US-specific (CLIA '88, OSHA, CMS/Medicare billing, FDA, AABB, Ch. 1 and 13). - **needs-verification** · outdated · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #12 — 'Twelve states plus Puerto Rico require lab personnel licensure' matches a university program list dated June 2017 (CA, FL, GA, HI, LA, MT, NV, NY, ND, RI, TN, WV + PR). The count can change and should be re-checked against current state law. _(evidence: [University of Minnesota, Medical Laboratory Sciences: Certification and Licensure (list current as of June 2017)](https://www.pharmacy.umn.edu/medical-laboratory-sciences/program-overview/certification-and-licensure))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #13 — 'Rooms >100 sq ft need two exits' and the HVAC targets (68–76 °F, 20–60% humidity, 12 air changes/hour) could not be matched to a consulted code or standard; exit and ventilation requirements depend on the fire and building codes and design standards in force.
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 1 · High-Yield Hits #11 — '~25 sharps injuries per 100 average daily census (2014)' and 'up to $5,000 per incident' were not found in the sources consulted. A Massachusetts surveillance report gives 16.2 per 100 licensed beds for 2014 (a different denominator). _(evidence: [Massachusetts DPH. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 3 · Exam Traps #1, #2 — Presents EDTA carry-over from wrong order of draw as certain. The order itself matches WHO 2010, but a closed-system study (Ercan 2021) found no detectable K-EDTA contamination from incorrect order in routine workflow; contamination is still reported in practice (Lima-Oliveira 2014 cites it as the cause of 14.3% of hypocalcaemia cases). The reviewer also omits that skin punctures reverse the order. _(evidence: [WHO guidelines on drawing blood (2010), Table 2.3 and section 7.1.3](https://iris.who.int/handle/10665/44294); [Ercan Ş et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2021;31(2):020704](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8047790); [Lima-Oliveira G et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2014;24(3):359-367](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4210256/))_
- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 3 · High-Yield Hits #7 — States fluoride 'good for 3 days'. WHO 2010 (Table 2.3) says oxalate/fluoride 'preserves glucose up to five days'. The reviewer also omits citrate-buffered tubes, which act immediately and are advised by AACC/ADA when samples cannot be cooled and separated within 30 minutes (as quoted in Gupta 2016). _(evidence: [WHO guidelines on drawing blood (2010), Table 2.3](https://iris.who.int/handle/10665/44294); [Gupta S et al. Int J Appl Basic Med Res. 2016;6(1):50-53](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4765275))_

'~1 in 1,000 specimens misidentified' and WBIT 1.1 vs 2.7 per 1,000 were not found in sources read; one large hospital reported 0.0511% (≈1 in 2,000) before interventions. _(evidence: [Bonini P et al. Clin Chem. 2002;48(5):691-698 (abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11978595/); [Linden JV et al. - **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 3 · High-Yield Hits #13 — Cites only the FDA 2017 biotin safety communication. FDA issued an update on November 5, 2019 and maintains a page listing troponin assays that have not addressed biotin interference (reports of falsely low troponin). The reviewer also omits the direction of error (sandwich assays falsely low, competitive assays falsely high). _(evidence: [FDA. Biotin interference with troponin lab tests — assays subject to biotin interference](https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/in-vitro-diagnostics/biotin-interference-troponin-lab-tests-assays-subject-biotin-interference); [Fan J et al. BCMJ. 2019;61(2):66-71](https://www.bcmj.org/articles/when-vitamin-supplementation-leads-harm-growing-popularity-biotin-and-its-impact-laboratory))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 3 · High-Yield Hits #15 — States 'first-morning void is best for cytology'. Reference-laboratory instructions state a first-morning voided specimen is not suitable for urine cytology and prefer a second-morning specimen; first-morning urine is preferred for chemical analysis (Caleffi 2010). The urobilinogen 2–4 pm timing was not verified. _(evidence: [Labcorp. Urine cytology (test 009068) specimen information](https://labcorp.com/tests/009068/urine-cytology); [Caleffi A et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2010;20(2):179-183](https://www.biochemia-medica.com/en/journal/20/2/10.11613/BM.2010.022/fullArticle))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 3 · Number Vault — Number Vault gives heel lancet depth '≤2 mm'. WHO 2010 (7.1.2) says heel-prick depth should not go beyond 2.4 mm and advises avoiding the posterior heel (2.33 mm to bone in a 3-kg baby). The 2 mm figure may come from another guideline that was not consulted. _(evidence: [WHO guidelines on drawing blood (2010), section 7.1.2](https://iris.who.int/handle/10665/44294))_
- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 3 · Number Vault — Number Vault gives 'arterial gas transport 1–5 °C ice water'. Sources disagree: WHO 2010 places the syringe in an ice cup, while Sood 2010 says do not cool and analyse within 30 minutes. Practice depends on syringe type and lab policy. _(evidence: [WHO guidelines on drawing blood (2010), section 5.2.2](https://iris.who.int/handle/10665/44294); [Sood P et al. Indian J Crit Care Med. 2010;14(2):57-64](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2936733))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 33 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States 'serum ferritin <12 μg/L is diagnostic' and that in inflammation '<50–60 μg/L predicts likely response to iron'. WHO 2020 recommends <15 µg/L for iron deficiency in apparently healthy children over 5, adolescents and adults, and <70 µg/L in adults (<30 µg/L in children) with infection or inflammation. Weiss 2019 associates ferritin <30 µg/L with true iron deficiency. Cut-offs are guideline-dependent. _(evidence: [WHO guideline on use of ferritin concentrations to assess iron status (2020), recommendations 1.1–1.2 and Table 1](https://iris.who.int/items/47948946-8d86-461c-9f31-aa49155337f2); [Weiss G, Ganz T, Goodnough LT. Blood. 2019;133(1):40-50](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6536698/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 57 · Exam Traps #2 — States the tube coagulase test is '~100% sensitive'. The ASM protocol calls the tube test definitive but gives no sensitivity figure; no source read for this app supports ~100%. _(evidence: [Katz DS. Coagulase Test Protocol. American Society for Microbiology; 2010 (revised 2016)](https://asm.org/protocols/coagulase-test-protocol))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 reads 'tube coagulase … positive = S. aureus'. ASM notes S. schleiferi and S. intermedius may also be tube-positive (and S. lugdunensis and S. schleiferi slide-positive). Hit 21 partly qualifies this ('clinically relevant coagulase-positive staphylococcus'). _(evidence: [Katz DS. Coagulase Test Protocol. American Society for Microbiology; 2010 (revised 2016)](https://asm.org/protocols/coagulase-test-protocol))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #8 — States that after a fever rise of ≥1 °C the transfusion must never be restarted until evaluated, and that febrile non-hemolytic reactions last 2–3 h. WHO 2001 (Clinical Use of Blood) allows restarting slowly with a new unit if the patient improves; the CDC NHSN Hemovigilance protocol v3.0 (2026) no longer defines febrile non-hemolytic reactions. The ≥1 °C threshold and 2–3 h course were not found in the sources read. _(evidence: [WHO. The Clinical Use of Blood: Handbook. 2001, pp. 61–67](https://iris.who.int/items/41b7dfd0-1fe8-43b6-bfbc-add30625693a); [CDC NHSN Biovigilance Component Hemovigilance Module Protocol v3.0, January 2026](https://www.cdc.gov/nhsn/pdfs/biovigilance/bv-hv-protocol-current.pdf))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 15 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States FENa > 1% suggests acute tubular necrosis. The 2025 European Journal of Heart Failure consensus (Table 2) treats FeNa 1–2% as indeterminate and > 2% as intrinsic; it and a 2026 BMJ Open meta-analysis also note that diuretics push FeNa above 1% even in prerenal states. _(evidence: [Kobayashi M, et al. Eur J Heart Fail. 2025;27(11):2372-2396, Table 2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12765416/); [Pan HC, et al. BMJ Open. 2026;16:e100875](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12815071/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States two blood-culture sets catch 80%, three 96% and four 100% of bacteremias (also in the Number Vault). These are Cockerill 2004 figures; Lee 2007 found 73.1%, 89.7%, 98.2% and 99.8% for one to four cultures. No source shows four sets detect 100%. _(evidence: [Lee A, Mirrett S, Reller LB, Weinstein MP. J Clin Microbiol. 2007;45(11):3546-3548 (abstract)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17881544/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States blood-culture sets are drawn 30–60 minutes apart (also in the Number Vault). CDC guidance says at least two sets within a few hours of each other (within 24 h) from separate venipuncture sites, and its worked example uses sets drawn within 5 minutes; no fixed 30–60-minute interval was found. _(evidence: [CDC. Blood Culture Contamination: An Overview for Infection Control and Antibiotic Stewardship Programs. 2022 (rev. 2023)](https://www.cdc.gov/antibiotic-use/core-elements/pdfs/fs-bloodculture-508.pdf); [CDC. Collect Adult Blood Culture Sets. Updated 2026 Mar 31](https://www.cdc.gov/labquality/blood-culture-contamination-prevention.html))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #10 — States HBV becomes chronic in 80% of neonates and 1–2% of adolescents/adults. CDC (2025) gives about 90% of infected infants, 30% of children infected at 1–5 years and about 5% of adults (95% recover); Kumar 2023 gives ~95% perinatal, 20–30% at 1–5 years, < 5% adults. _(evidence: [CDC. Clinical Overview of Hepatitis B. Updated 2025 Aug 29](https://www.cdc.gov/hepatitis-b/hcp/clinical-overview/index.html); [Kumar M, et al. Diagnostics (Basel). 2023;13(3):368](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9914745))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Calls IgM anti-HBc the 'sole marker' of the core window. Total anti-HBc (which includes IgM) is also positive in that phase (Patel 2024, Table 2), and HBV DNA can be detected in the window period (Kumar 2023). The practical point — test IgM anti-HBc — is correct. _(evidence: [Patel A, et al. Gastro Hep Adv. 2024;3(2):139, Table 2](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC11307719); [Kumar M, et al. Diagnostics (Basel). 2023;13(3):368, Table 2](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9914745))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 64 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States multiplex viral NAATs are 'the standard of care for respiratory viruses'. The IDSA 2018 influenza guideline recommends molecular assays over antigen tests broadly, but recommends multiplex RT-PCR respiratory panels specifically for hospitalized immunocompromised patients. Other pathogens and newer guidance were not checked. _(evidence: [Uyeki TM, et al. Clin Infect Dis. 2019;68(6):e1-e47, recommendations 10–12](https://academic.oup.com/cid/article/68/6/e1/5251935))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 42 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Gives removal as Y = Y₀·e⁻ˣ but states 30% remains after one plasma volume and 10% after two; e⁻¹ ≈ 37% and e⁻² ≈ 14%. Bauer 2022 reports the first session removes 65–70% of an intravascular substance, the second about 23% more and the third about 9% more. _(evidence: [Bauer PR, et al. Intensive Care Med. 2022;48(10):1382](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9372988))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 43 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Gives 2.5 × 10⁶ CD34⁺ cells/kg as the minimal engraftment dose. De Clercq 2019 cites a minimum threshold of ≥ 2 × 10⁶ CD34⁺/kg for autologous PBSC transplantation. Thresholds differ by program and transplant type; the reviewer's figure was not found in the sources read. _(evidence: [De Clercq E. Antivir Chem Chemother. 2019;27:2040206619829382](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC6379795))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 24 · Exam Traps #3 — Defines a dilute specimen as creatinine ≤ 20 mg/dL and SG ≤ 1.0030. The US DOT laboratory criteria (49 CFR 40.88) are creatinine ≥ 2 and < 20 mg/dL with SG > 1.0010 and < 1.0030; values below those floors fall in the substituted or invalid range. Argoff 2018 uses ≤ 20 mg/dL and ≤ 1.003 as clinical tampering flags, so the reviewer’s numbers match clinical screening practice but not the regulatory definition. _(evidence: [49 CFR Part 40 (eCFR), §§ 40.65, 40.87, 40.88](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-49/subtitle-A/part-40); [Argoff CE, et al. Pain Med. 2018;19(1):97-117](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC6516588))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 24 · Exam Traps #3 — Lists “temperature outside 90–100 °F” among the criteria for a substituted specimen. Under 49 CFR 40.65, temperature (32–38 °C / 90–100 °F, read within 4 minutes) is a collection-site check; an out-of-range reading triggers an immediate new collection under direct observation, and both specimens are sent. “Substituted” is a laboratory result based on creatinine and specific gravity (40.88). _(evidence: [49 CFR Part 40 (eCFR), §§ 40.65, 40.87, 40.88](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-49/subtitle-A/part-40))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #4 — States BNP “does NOT rise” in TRALI and that TRALI gets “oxygen only”. The 2019 consensus redefinition says natriuretic peptides are always elevated in critically ill patients and severe TRALI (hypoxic vasoconstriction), so they help mainly in milder cases; a low BNP (< 300 pg/mL) or NT-proBNP (< 2,000 pg/mL) argues against TACO. TRALI care is supportive and includes mechanical ventilation when needed; avoiding diuretics is supported. _(evidence: [Vlaar APJ, et al. Transfusion. 2019;59(7):2465-2476](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC6850655); [Kuriri FA. Saudi Med J. 2025;46(8):865-877](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC12392415))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 18 · Exam Traps #2 — States a direct (homogeneous) LDL-C assay “stays valid even at TG 600” when Friedewald cannot be used. HEART UK/ACB (Kenkre 2025) reports marked, clinically relevant bias of direct LDL-c versus the CDC reference method, says performance is method-dependent and does not recommend direct LDL-c in hypertriglyceridaemic samples (suggesting apoB); Wolska 2026 notes direct assays on dyslipidaemic samples can be substantially lower or higher than beta-quantification. Non-HDL-C, Sampson–NIH (to TG 800 mg/dL) or extended Martin–Hopkins are the supported alternatives. _(evidence: [Kenkre JS, et al. Ann Clin Biochem. 2025;62(4):257-286](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC12227826); [Wolska A, et al. Curr Atheroscler Rep. 2026;28(1):27](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC12935843))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 40 · High-Yield Hits #7 — States enzyme treatment (ficin/papain) destroys S/s along with Fyᵃ/Fyᵇ, M and N. Manduzio 2024 states S and s show a variable effect with enzymes (M, N and Duffy destroyed; Kell unaffected). Effects may differ between papain and ficin and between methods; a second source was not read. _(evidence: [Manduzio P. Hematol Rep. 2024;16(4):815-824](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC11675097))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Presents a fixed hCG discriminatory zone of 1500–2000 mIU/mL above which a sac “should” be seen, with no sac suggesting ectopic. Bobdiwala 2017 says the zone should not be used to time scans or to make a presumptive diagnosis of ectopic pregnancy, that most ectopics are seen at hCG < 1,000 IU/L, and that basing methotrexate on it is dangerous; Pereira 2019 notes the value varies by centre and equipment (their protocol uses 3,500 mIU/mL). _(evidence: [Bobdiwala S, et al. Womens Health (Lond). 2017;13(2):27-33](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC5557179); [Pereira PP, et al. Clinics (Sao Paulo). 2019;74:e1111](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC6784610))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Uses “rising < 53% in 2 days = abnormal (99% sensitive)”. A later study of 1,249 PUL patients set the minimal 48-h rise for a viable intrauterine pregnancy at 35% (99.9% CI); the 53% threshold can misclassify viable pregnancies (Pereira 2019; Nwabuobi 2017). _(evidence: [Pereira PP, et al. Clinics (Sao Paulo). 2019;74:e1111](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC6784610); [Nwabuobi C, et al. Int J Mol Sci. 2017;18(10):2037](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC5666719))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #10 — States platelets are stored “up to 5 days” (also in the Number Vault and simulator explanation). Room-temperature platelet storage is now 5–7 days where bacterial-risk controls (e.g. pathogen reduction) are used, and an FDA 2023 variance allows cold-stored platelets for up to 14 days at 1–6 °C for active bleeding (Akaraphanth 2026; Cancelas 2022). _(evidence: [Akaraphanth M, et al. Transfusion. 2026;66(Suppl 1):S350-S367](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13184439); [Cancelas JA, et al. Transfusion. 2022;62(8):1619-1629](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9546462))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #10 — States end-of-storage platelet pH “must be ≥ 6.0”. A 2022 US licensing study used pH (22 °C) ≥ 6.2 as the acceptance endpoint (Cancelas 2022). The current AABB/FDA criterion was not read directly, so this stays unconfirmed. _(evidence: [Cancelas JA, et al. Transfusion. 2022;62(8):1619-1629](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9546462))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 25 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States acromegaly “is diagnosed by” failure of GH to suppress below 1 ng/mL during an OGTT. The 2024 Acromegaly Consensus confirms the diagnosis with IGF-I > 1.3 × ULN for age plus characteristic clinical signs; random GH is not required, and the OGTT is reserved for equivocal results (Giustina 2024; De Marinis 2026). _(evidence: [Giustina A, et al. Pituitary. 2024;27(1):7-22](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC10837217); [De Marinis L, et al. Pituitary. 2026;29(2):67](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13038677))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 62 · High-Yield Hits #3 — States chlamydiae are “energy parasites” with no cytochromes that “can’t make ATP”. Current reviews: reticulate bodies scavenge host ATP, but elementary bodies generate ATP from glucose-6-phosphate and amino acids, and C. trachomatis can make ATP via a sodium gradient — the 1960s–70s energy-parasite hypothesis has been challenged (Mandel 2024; Cheong 2023). _(evidence: [Mandel CG, et al. Front Cell Infect Microbiol. 2024;14:1284701](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC10995303); [Cheong HC, et al. Microorganisms. 2023;11(6):1382](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC10301204))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 62 · Exam Traps #4 — Presents azithromycin 1 g single dose and doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 7 days as equivalent first-line options for genital chlamydia. The CDC 2021 STI guidelines recommend doxycycline for 7 days; azithromycin 1 g is an alternative regimen (Workowski 2021). _(evidence: [Workowski KA, et al. MMWR Recomm Rep. 2021;70(4):1-187](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC8344968))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 33 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Lists “osmotic fragility increased” alongside EMA binding as the HS tests without noting that the conventional osmotic fragility test is no longer recommended (low sensitivity and specificity, poor standardisation); flow-based osmotic fragility and EMA binding are preferred (Chueh 2022 Korean guideline). _(evidence: [Chueh HW, et al. Blood Res. 2022;57(2):86-94](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC9242826))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 23 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Defines acute pancreatitis as “clinical features + amylase/lipase >3× the upper reference limit”. The revised Atlanta classification requires at least two of three — typical abdominal pain, amylase and/or lipase ≥ 3 × ULN, characteristic imaging — so enzyme elevation is not mandatory (Varlamov 2026; Lázár 2026). _(evidence: [Varlamov D, et al. BMC Gastroenterol. 2026;26(1):561](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13576186); [Lázár B, et al. Front Cell Infect Microbiol. 2026;16:1808503](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13384862))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 63 · Exam Traps #5 — States “morulae in NEUTROPHILS = Anaplasma phagocytophilum; Ehrlichia targets MONOCYTES.” CDC notes Ehrlichia ewingii has a predilection for granulocytes and forms morulae there, and that smear findings need confirmatory testing (Biggs 2016). _(evidence: [Biggs HM, et al. MMWR Recomm Rep. 2016;65(2):1-44](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/65/rr/rr6502a1.htm))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Gives a red-cell trigger of “< 8.0 g/dL in acute coronary syndrome”. The MINT trial (acute MI, n = 3504) found a restrictive 7–8 g/dL strategy associated with more MI or death than a liberal 10 g/dL strategy (HR 1.15), so a fixed restrictive trigger in acute MI is contested (Yang 2026). _(evidence: [Yang H, et al. Front Med (Lausanne). 2026;13:1762664](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13008982))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Gives cryoprecipitate when fibrinogen < 100 mg/dL with bleeding, and ~150–250 mg fibrinogen per unit. Current reviews put increased bleeding risk below ~200 mg/dL (population-dependent), trials enrol at < 200 mg/dL, and the AABB standard is ≥ 150 mg fibrinogen per unit (US single units often ~700 mg per American Red Cross) (Rahe-Meyer 2026; Hess 2026). _(evidence: [Rahe-Meyer N, et al. J Clin Med. 2026;15(16):6156](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13512969); [Hess AS, et al. Transfusion. 2026;66(7):1424-1437](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13350501))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 36 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Gives mild hemophilia as 6–30% factor activity. The standard classification is severe < 1 IU/dL, moderate 1–5, mild > 5–40 IU/dL (Alvarez-Payares 2026, citing the ISTH/WFH classification). _(evidence: [Alvarez-Payares JC, et al. Cureus. 2026;18(4):e106284](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13134844))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 19 · High-Yield Hits #1, #3, #5 — Built on the Fourth Universal Definition of MI (2018) with numbered MI types (e.g., type 4a, type 5) and sex-specific cut-offs only “when provided”. The Fifth Universal Definition (2026) requires sex-specific 99th percentile URLs and replaces the numbered types with primary, secondary and procedure-related MI (Mills 2026). _(evidence: [Mills NL, et al. Glob Heart. 2026;21(1):64](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13523810))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 58 · Exam Traps #5 — States that against metallo-β-lactamase producers “aztreonam stays active”. MBLs cannot hydrolyse aztreonam, but many MBL producers co-express ESBL and/or AmpC enzymes that inactivate it, which is why aztreonam is combined with avibactam (Khan 2026). _(evidence: [Khan M, et al. Front Microbiol. 2026;17:1913726](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC13531602))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Presents nitrocefin as the way to catch β-lactamase hidden behind a susceptible MIC in staphylococci. In a blaZ-confirmed study (S. saprophyticus) nitrocefin was the least sensitive phenotypic test (28.9%) and the zone-edge test the most sensitive (90.3%); combined testing performed best (Ferreira 2017). Generalisability to S. aureus was not verified. _(evidence: [Ferreira AM, et al. Braz J Microbiol. 2017;48(1):159-166](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC5221353))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 42 · High-Yield Hits #15, #16 — States the 12.5 g/dL / 38% hemoglobin minimum “applies to ALL whole-blood donors”. Under 21 CFR 630.10, 12.5 g/dL / 38% is the female allogeneic minimum (12.0–12.5 permitted under an FDA-acceptable procedure); male allogeneic donors need ≥ 13.0 g/dL / 39%. The checklist also omits the lower BP limits (systolic ≥ 90, diastolic ≥ 50 mmHg). _(evidence: [21 CFR 630.10 (eCFR, accessed 2026-10-08)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-21/part-630/section-630.10))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #29 — Calls Enterococcus a “bacitracin-resistant + PYR-positive beta-hemolytic streptococcus”. Enterococcus is a separate genus, and most isolates are not β-hemolytic (β-hemolysis in 20.3% of 74 isolates in one 2026 study, on horse blood). The PYR/bacitracin answer stands, but identification should rest on growth in 6.5% NaCl and a positive bile esculin test, not on β-hemolysis. _(evidence: [Fialho N, et al. Antibiotics (Basel). 2026;15(7):657](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13404461/); [Gajic I, et al. Int J Mol Sci. 2026;27(11):5092 (Table 4)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13256776/))_
- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 43 · High-Yield Hits #12 — States that “HPCs do not express ABO antigens (they engraft in ABO-incompatible recipients)”. Matteocci 2024 states that ABO antigens are present on hematopoietic stem cells. The clinical conclusion (ABO incompatibility is not a contraindication; incompatible red cells or plasma in the product are reduced) is supported, but the mechanism given conflicts with the source read. Primary studies of ABO expression on CD34⁺ cells were not read. _(evidence: [Matteocci A, Pierelli L. Cells. 2024;13(10):814](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11119197/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 37 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Uses a VWF activity/antigen ratio of ≥ 0.6 vs < 0.6 to separate type 1 from type 2, and (Memory Hooks) a FVIII:C/VWF:Ag ratio < 0.6 for type 2N. The ASH ISTH NHF WFH 2021 guideline, as summarized by Tosetto & Eikenboom 2026, suggests < 0.7 to confirm type 2 (low certainty) and uses FVIII/VWF:Ag < 0.7 to prompt testing for 2N or mild hemophilia A. Cut-offs remain lab- and assay-dependent. _(evidence: [Tosetto A, Eikenboom J. Haematologica. 2026;111(1):35-43](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12775743/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 15 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Gives the normal anion gap as “≈ 12 mEq/L (8–16)”. That range dates from flame-photometry sodium and colorimetric chloride; ion-selective electrodes shifted the normal gap to about 6 ± 3 mEq/L (Lee 2006), and a 2025 review lists 4–12 mmol/L. The normal range is method-dependent and should be taken from the performing laboratory. _(evidence: [Lee S, Kang KP, Kang SK. Electrolyte Blood Press. 2006;4(1):44-46](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3894543/); [Korus J, et al. Diagnostics (Basel). 2025;15(16):2052](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12385983/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Says sputum is “rejected if > 10 squamous epithelial cells/LPF” but “acceptable if < 25 epithelial cells AND > 25 neutrophils/LPF” — a specimen with 11–24 SEC would be both rejected and acceptable. The criterion read (Dong 2026) is acceptable with < 10 SEC and > 25 leukocytes per LPF; > 10 SEC indicates oropharyngeal contamination. Some scoring systems differ; laboratories set their own criteria. _(evidence: [Dong K, et al. Front Microbiol. 2026;17:1894814](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13385188/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 41 · High-Yield Hits #14 — States that the HBV “core window” (HBsAg cleared, anti-HBs not yet present) “is caught by HBV NAT — why NAT is in the required donor screening panel”. Badawi 2025 describes the core-window gap as the period when anti-HBc may be the only positive marker; NAT’s documented role is shortening the early window (HBV DNA detectable on average 6–15 days before HBsAg) and detecting occult infection. The reviewer’s own simulator (Q190, Q579) correctly ties the core window to anti-HBc. _(evidence: [Badawi MA, et al. Viruses. 2025;18(1):23](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12846598/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 29 · High-Yield Hits #1 — States “RBC casts and dysmorphic RBCs = glomerular disease, full stop.” RBC casts are usually glomerular (nephritic syndrome), but a biopsy study cited by Gaggar 2024 found RBC casts in 29% of acute interstitial nephritis; acanthocytes and dysmorphic RBCs are relatively specific but insensitive markers. _(evidence: [Gaggar P, Raju SB. Indian J Nephrol. 2024;34(3):213-221](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11303840/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 29 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Ties waxy and broad casts to end-stage kidney disease and WBC casts to pyelonephritis. Gaggar 2024 lists waxy casts in renal failure, acute as well as chronic, and WBC casts in acute interstitial nephritis and nephritic syndrome as well as pyelonephritis. _(evidence: [Gaggar P, Raju SB. Indian J Nephrol. 2024;34(3):213-221](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11303840/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 34 · High-Yield Hits #4 — States PV has “JAK2 V617F in essentially all cases”. JAK2 V617F is found in > 95% of PV; the remaining 3–5% carry non-canonical JAK2 mutations (exons 12–15), which the WHO/ICC 2022 criteria accept. A V617F-negative result therefore does not exclude PV. _(evidence: [Palandri F, et al. Ann Hematol. 2026;105(5):251](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13091898/); [Testa U, et al. Med Sci (Basel). 2026;14(5):552](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13609502/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 34 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Gives PMF “median survival ~5 years”. Shao 2025 cites an overall median survival of 9.2 years (15- and 20-year survival 32% and 20%); survival varies widely with risk score. _(evidence: [Shao R, et al. Cancers (Basel). 2025;18(1):50](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12784778/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 38 · High-Yield Hits #3, #9 — Hit 3 gives homozygous factor V Leiden ~10–15× VTE risk and Hit 9 / the Number Vault give FVL heterozygote + OCPs 8–20×, while the reviewer’s own Ch. 72 simulator question and explanation cite ~7× heterozygous and ~80× homozygous. Published figures differ: Khider 2022 gives ~5× heterozygous and ~10× homozygous; Nakashima & Rogers 2014 give 4–8× heterozygous, 80× homozygous and 30–60× for heterozygotes on oral contraceptives. _(evidence: [Nakashima MO, Rogers HJ. Blood Res. 2014;49(2):85-94](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4090343/); [Khider L, Gendron N, Mauge L. Int J Mol Sci. 2022;23(3):1821](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8837096/))_
- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 20 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 and the “Hapto-goblin” memory hook say myoglobin does not bind haptoglobin. Two rhabdomyolysis reviews state that circulating myoglobin is normally bound to plasma globulins, mainly haptoglobin. The diagnostic contrast (low haptoglobin in hemolysis; very high CK in rhabdomyolysis) is not in question, but the binding claim needs a primary binding study. _(evidence: [Petejova N, Martinek A. Crit Care. 2014;18(3):224](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4056317/); [Gupta A, Thorson P, Penmatsa KR, Gupta P. Ulster Med J. 2021;90(2):61-69](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8278949/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 20 · Exam Traps #2 — Trap 2 calls bisalbuminemia a benign heterozygous variant (slower band) needing no work-up. Hereditary bisalbuminemia is permanent and benign, but an acquired, transient form with a faster band occurs with high-dose β-lactam antibiotics and pancreatic pseudocyst. _(evidence: [Kapatia G, et al. J Lab Physicians. 2021;13(3):219-223](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8478498/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 60 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 and Trap 4 present piperacillin-tazobactam as a current cause of false-positive galactomannan (“FDA says disclose it”). The false positives came from galactomannan-contaminated lots; a 2014 multicentre US study found no galactomannan in 32 current lots and 1 of 27 patient sera (3.7%) positive, concluding that current US formulations are a rare cause. Formulations outside the US were not checked. _(evidence: [Vergidis P, et al. J Clin Microbiol. 2014;52(6):2199-2201 (abstract)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4042793/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 60 · Exam Simulator #786 — Ch. 57 Hit 33 and simulator question 786 describe Aspergillus niger as having uniseriate phialides with smooth conidia. Samson 2007 lists A. niger among the biseriate species of section Nigri; the uniseriate black aspergilli are A. japonicus, A. aculeatus and relatives. _(evidence: [Samson RA, et al. Diagnostic tools to identify black aspergilli. Stud Mycol. 2007;59:129-145](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2275192/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 40 · High-Yield Hits #10, #15 — Hit 10, Hit 15 and Trap 5 say anti-K titers underestimate fetal risk and call for MCA Doppler “regardless of titer.” A 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis found that a maternal anti-K titer ≥ 8 had 97% sensitivity for severe HDFN needing intrauterine intervention, with 98.6% of severe cases at titers ≥ 8, and suggested guidelines be re-evaluated. Anti-K does suppress fetal erythropoiesis; guidance on critical titers differs. _(evidence: [Jacobs JW, et al. Transfus Med Rev. 2025;39(2):150895](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13180262/); [Valentini B, et al. Pregnancy (Hoboken). 2025;1(5):e70085](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13344805/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 40 · Exam Simulator #372 — Simulator question 372 gives 2 vials for a 60 mL fetal whole-blood bleed (60 ÷ 30). Technical guidance cited by Hajjaj 2025 rounds the quotient (up if ≥ 0.5) and then adds one vial to cover maternal blood-volume and counting error, giving 3 vials; a Canadian survey shows practice varies. _(evidence: [Hajjaj OI, et al. Br J Haematol. 2025;207(3):723-736](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12436229/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #36 — Hit 36 lists HACEK as the agents of “culture-negative subacute” endocarditis. HACEK organisms are slow-growing and CO₂-enhanced, but an automated blood-culture system detected ~99% within the standard 5 days, and prolonged incubation added no value (as summarized by Khaledi 2022). _(evidence: [Khaledi M, et al. J Cardiothorac Surg. 2022;17(1):185](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9389832/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 36 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Hit 14 and Trap 6 say to keep platelets > 20 × 10⁹/L in bleeding DIC. The BCSH, JSTH and SISET guidelines and their ISTH harmonization, as summarized by Wada 2014, transfuse platelets in bleeding DIC at ≤ 50 × 10⁹/L and use 10–20 × 10⁹/L only for non-bleeding patients (e.g., after chemotherapy). _(evidence: [Wada H, Matsumoto T, Yamashita Y. J Intensive Care. 2014;2(1):15](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4267589/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 17 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 and Trap 3 state that high insulin + high C-peptide = insulinoma. Sulfonylurea (and meglitinide) ingestion produces the same endogenous pattern; ENETS 2012 lists a negative sulfonylurea screen among the diagnostic criteria, and Peltola 2018 notes a negative oral hypoglycemic agent screen is needed to establish endogenous hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia. _(evidence: [Jensen RT, et al. Neuroendocrinology. 2012;95(2):98-119](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3701449/); [Peltola E, et al. Int J Endocrinol. 2018;2018:2059481](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6218736/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 42 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 says plerixafor is FDA-approved for NHL/multiple myeloma patients “failing G-CSF mobilization.” De Clercq 2019 describes the December 2008 FDA approval for autologous mobilization in NHL or multiple myeloma, given with G-CSF; rescue after poor mobilization is one use, not the approved population. The FDA label itself was not read. _(evidence: [De Clercq E. Antivir Chem Chemother. 2019;27:2040206619829382](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6379795/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 30 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 defines septic arthritis as synovial WBC 50,000–200,000/µL with > 90% PMNs, low glucose and a positive Gram stain/culture. Long 2019 notes counts ≥ 50,000/µL also occur in gout and pseudogout, nearly half of culture-proven septic joints have ≤ 28,000/µL, synovial protein and glucose do not meaningfully change the likelihood, and Gram stain sensitivity is only ~29–65% for gram-positive infection. _(evidence: [Long B, Koyfman A, Gottlieb M. West J Emerg Med. 2019;20(2):331-341](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6404712/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 (and Table 1) classes HTT 36–39 CAG repeats as “intermediate”. Nopoulos 2016 and Chintalaphani 2021 classify 36–39 as reduced (incomplete/partial) penetrance — some carriers develop Huntington disease — while 27–35 is the range that causes no disease but may expand on transmission. _(evidence: [Nopoulos PC. Dialogues Clin Neurosci. 2016;18(1):91-98](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4826775/); [Chintalaphani SR, et al. Acta Neuropathol Commun. 2021;9(1):98](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8145836/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 says congenital myotonic dystrophy type 1 (1000–2000 CTG) is transmitted “MATERNALLY only”. Wenninger 2018: congenital DM1 expansions (> 800, usually ~1,000) are characteristically maternal, but congenital DM1 with paternal transmission is also known. Wenninger also gives 5–37 as healthy and > 50 as fully penetrant, not the reviewer’s 5–35 and > 100. _(evidence: [Wenninger S, Montagnese F, Schoser B. Front Neurol. 2018;9:303](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5941986/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #40 — Hit 40 says to detect Shiga toxin directly in stool by EIA (or NAAT). Humphries & Linscott 2015 and CDC 2009 recommend Shiga toxin immunoassays on overnight enrichment broth (or primary-plate growth); direct-stool EIA has much lower sensitivity (~70%). NAAT for stx genes and simultaneous O157 culture remain appropriate (IDSA 2017). _(evidence: [Humphries RM, Linscott AJ. Clin Microbiol Rev. 2015;28(1):3-31](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4284301/); [Gould LH, et al. MMWR Recomm Rep. 2009;58(RR-12):1-14](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/preview/mmwrhtml/rr5812a1.htm))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 64 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Hit 14 says p24 antigen is the earliest detectable HIV marker. CDC 2014: HIV-1 RNA becomes detectable by NAT about 10 days after infection, and p24 antigen becomes detectable by 4th-generation assays 4–10 days after RNA. p24 precedes antibodies but is not the earliest marker. _(evidence: [CDC/APHL. Laboratory Testing for the Diagnosis of HIV Infection: Updated Recommendations. 2014](https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/23447))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 16 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 uses 4.4 g/dL as the reference albumin in the corrected-calcium formula (Ca + 0.8 × (4.4 − albumin)), and the simulator calls 4.0 g/dL “outdated”. Desgagnés 2025 and Mirrakhimov 2015 give the commonly used simplified Payne formula with 4.0 g/dL (40 g/L). Desgagnés 2025 also found unadjusted total calcium agreed with ionized calcium at least as well as adjusted calcium. _(evidence: [Desgagnés N, et al. JAMA Netw Open. 2025;8(1):e2455251](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11751745/); [Mirrakhimov AE. N Am J Med Sci. 2015;7(11):483-493](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4683803/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 (and the Number Vault and memory section) states “MR2 = 10%” and describes each deep response as ~1.5 logs deeper. ELN 2020: 1%, 0.1%, 0.01%, 0.0032% and 0.001% IS correspond to 2, 3, 4, 4.5 and 5 logs below baseline, with MR³ (MMR), MR⁴ and MR⁴·⁵ named by their log reduction — so MR² corresponds to 1% IS; 10% is a 1-log reduction. _(evidence: [Hochhaus A, et al. Leukemia. 2020;34(4):966-984](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7214240/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 76 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Hit 1 (and Figure 76, Memory Hooks, Number Vault) gives oncoprotein assays a blanket 95–100% specificity, so that a positive “strongly implies malignancy”. Specificity is marker- and setting-specific: the NMP22 BladderChek test, listed in the same chapter, had pooled specificity 88% (sensitivity 56%) in a 19-study meta-analysis, and cystoscopy remains the gold standard. _(evidence: [Wang Z, et al. Oncotarget. 2017;8(59):100648-100656](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5725051/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 76 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 (and the Number Vault) states KRAS mutations in “~75% of colon cancers”. Simanshu 2017 Table 1 gives KRAS 42% for colon (pancreas 91%, consistent with the reviewer’s >90%). Frequencies vary by study, but ~75% is well above this tabulation. _(evidence: [Simanshu DK, Nissley DV, McCormick F. Cell. 2017;170(1):17-33](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5555610/))_
- **verified-issue** · uncertain · Ch. 73 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 and Trap 2 state that ACE inhibition “is a risk factor for cognitive impairment/AD onset”. A 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis (18 studies) found lower dementia risk with Ang-II stimulating antihypertensives than with Ang-II inhibiting ones (a class that includes ACE inhibitors) in observational studies, but the meta-analysis of RCTs was non-significant. A causal risk from ACE inhibitors is not established; the statement is presented as settled. _(evidence: [Belachew EA, Peterson GM, Bezabhe WM. Geroscience. 2025;47(4):5525-5541](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12397011/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 50 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 states that any positive direct CDC crossmatch with current recipient serum is an “absolute contraindication” to renal transplantation. Althaf 2017 grades positive crossmatches by antibody class and specificity: IgG HLA donor-specific antibody is the veto, but CDC positives that disappear with DTT (IgM, often autoreactive non-HLA) are standard risk, and lupus immune complexes can give false-positive B-cell CDC results (worked case: FCXM negative, no DSA on single-antigen beads). _(evidence: [Althaf MM, et al. World J Transplant. 2017;7(6):339-348](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5743871/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 52 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 states CVID diagnosis requires “age >2 years”. That is the 1999 ESID/PAGID definition. The 2016 International Consensus Document (ICON) advises that, because of the broad differential and the difficulty of assessing vaccine responses in early childhood, CVID is best not diagnosed before at least age 4 years. _(evidence: [Bonilla FA, et al. J Allergy Clin Immunol Pract. 2016;4(1):38-59](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4869529/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 55 · High-Yield Hits #1 — Hit 1 presents anti-endomysial antibody (anti-EMA) as the confirmation test (“nearly 100% specific for confirmation”). The ESsCD 2025 adult guideline states that routine IgA anti-EMA serology is no longer recommended for confirmation; adults are confirmed by duodenal biopsy or, conditionally, without biopsy if under 45 years with IgA anti-TG2 ≥ 10 × ULN confirmed in a second sample. _(evidence: [Al-Toma A, et al. United European Gastroenterol J. 2025;13(10):1855-1886](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12704582/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 states “relative risk RR = (a×d)/(b×c)”. That cross-product ratio is the odds ratio, the measure used for HLA–disease association in case-control studies (Ziade 2017 calculates the OR with the classic formula a*d/b*c). It is not a risk ratio. _(evidence: [Ziade NR. BMC Musculoskelet Disord. 2017;18(1):280](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5492927/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 gives HFE C282Y homozygote penetrance “as low as 1–10%”. Penetrance depends on how disease is defined; in the eMERGE network the clinical diagnostic rate of haemochromatosis among C282Y homozygotes was 24.4% in males and 14.0% in females. The reviewer’s point (most homozygotes never develop disease; no population screening) is consistent, but the figure should be qualified. _(evidence: [Gallego CJ, et al. Am J Hum Genet. 2015;97(4):512-520](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4596892/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 12 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 (and the matching Memory Hook and 5-Second Recall) defines Isolation as “concurrent transactions behave as sequential”. That describes only the strictest (serializable) isolation level; databases offer weaker levels and often default to one (PostgreSQL defaults to Read Committed). The general definition is that a transaction’s effects are not visible to concurrent transactions before it commits. _(evidence: [PostgreSQL Documentation (current): Glossary; Section 13.2 Transaction Isolation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/transaction-iso.html))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 13 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3, the Number Vault and the 5-Second Recall define contribution margin as price minus FULLY LOADED cost (PSA example: $41.54 − $34.62 = $6.92). Standard managerial accounting defines contribution margin as selling price minus VARIABLE cost per unit (its contribution toward fixed costs, then profit); price minus fully loaded cost is profit per unit. The reviewer may follow its source text’s usage, but candidates should know the standard definition. _(evidence: [Franklin M, Graybeal P, Cooper D. Principles of Accounting, Volume 2: Managerial Accounting. OpenStax; 2019. Section 3.1](https://openstax.org/books/principles-managerial-accounting/pages/3-1-explain-contribution-margin-and-calculate-contribution-margin-per-unit-contribution-margin-ratio-and-total-contribution-margin))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 13 · Exam Traps #4 — Trap 4 says a positive NPV is not enough and that IRR must also be checked against the cost of capital. Because IRR is the discount rate at which NPV = 0, for a conventional project (one outlay followed by inflows) a positive NPV at the cost of capital already means IRR exceeds that rate; the two tests agree on accept/reject. They can conflict only when ranking mutually exclusive projects or with unconventional cash flows. The reviewer’s own example (negative NPV, IRR 8% < 10%) is consistent. _(evidence: [Franklin M, Graybeal P, Cooper D. Principles of Accounting, Volume 2: Managerial Accounting. OpenStax; 2019. Section 11.4](https://openstax.org/books/principles-managerial-accounting/pages/11-4-use-discounted-cash-flow-models-to-make-capital-investment-decisions))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 14 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 and Trap 2 state that the IRB, not the investigator, decides exemption. The Common Rule text (45 CFR 46.104) lists exempt categories but does not say who makes the determination; that is set by institutional policy, and OHRP guidance (which the app could not fetch, HTTP 403) is commonly cited as recommending that investigators not decide alone. Many institutions assign it to the IRB office, but “the IRB, never the investigator” is a policy norm, not a regulatory requirement as far as the regulation text shows. _(evidence: [45 CFR 46.104 Exempt research (eCFR, version 2026-10-01)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-45/subtitle-A/subchapter-A/part-46/subpart-A/section-46.104))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Describes vanB as 'lower-level vancomycin resistance'. O'Driscoll 2015 (Table 1, adapted from Courvalin 2006) gives VanB vancomycin MICs of 4-1,000 mg/L with teicoplanin MICs of 0.5-1 mg/L. The level of vancomycin resistance varies widely, and the feature that separates it from vanA is teicoplanin susceptibility. _(evidence: [O'Driscoll T, et al. Infect Drug Resist. 2015;8:217-230](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4521680/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Gives a single streptomycin high-level screen of 1000 µg/mL. The cutoff depends on method: >1,000 µg/mL by broth dilution and >2,000 µg/mL by agar dilution (Shridhar 2019, citing CLSI). Adhikari 2010 uses a 2,000 µg/mL agar screen, and Miller 2014 defines high-level streptomycin resistance as MIC >2000 µg/mL. _(evidence: [Shridhar S, et al. Interdiscip Perspect Infect Dis. 2019;2019:7854968](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6420983/); [Adhikari L. J Glob Infect Dis. 2010;2(3):231-235](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2946678/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 58 · Exam Traps #3 — States that oxacillin susceptibility 'only rules out mecA'. Paterson 2014 reports that on the Vitek 2 system most mecC MRSA are cefoxitin-resistant but oxacillin-susceptible (other automated systems not fully tested), and that cefoxitin is more reliable than oxacillin in disc diffusion, broth microdilution and agar dilution. Oxacillin susceptibility alone therefore does not exclude methicillin resistance. Commercial mecA PCR and PBP2a slide agglutination tests miss mecC, so mecC PCR is needed to confirm. _(evidence: [Paterson GK, et al. Trends Microbiol. 2014;22(1):42-47](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3989053/))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Says to screen penicillin-susceptible S. aureus with nitrocefin. Takayama 2018 notes that CLSI/EUCAST recommend both nitrocefin and the penicillin zone-edge test, and that nitrocefin is reported less sensitive than the zone-edge test. In the study itself only 2 of 200 MSSA were blaZ-positive, and both tests performed poorly (nitrocefin sensitivity 50%, P10 zone-edge PPV 18.2%). A negative nitrocefin result alone does not rule out penicillinase. This extends rv-nitrocefin to S. aureus. _(evidence: [Takayama Y, et al. Ann Lab Med. 2018;38(2):155-159](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5736675/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 59 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 and the Number Vault give Xpert MTB/RIF sensitivity as 98-100% in smear-positive and 69-72% in smear-negative sputum, and the Number Vault gives specificity as 100%. The 2019 Cochrane meta-analysis gives pooled sensitivity of 98% (97-98%) in smear-positive and 67% (62-72%) in smear-negative, culture-positive adults, and pooled specificity of 98% (97-98%) in unselected participants. The landmark single study (Boehme 2010) reported 99.2% specificity for a single direct test, not 100%. _(evidence: [Horne DJ, et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2019;6:CD009593](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6555588/); [Boehme CC, et al. N Engl J Med. 2010;363(11):1005-1015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2947799/))_

- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 68 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 says Southern and northern blots are 'both largely replaced by amplification methods'. Open sources qualify this. Yang 2022 says northern blotting is still used as a gold standard for validating high-throughput expression data and uniquely gives transcript size and expression level, although qPCR is now often used. Rossetti 2011 says Southern blot is still considered the reference method for hemophilia A intron 22 inversion genotyping. Pandelache 2021 reports a Southern blot detecting fragile X full-mutation alleles that two PCR assays missed: AmplideX triplet-primed PCR and long-range methylation-sensitive PCR. Blots keep specific clinical and reference roles. _(evidence: [Yang T, Zhang M, Zhang N. BMC Genomics. 2022;23(1):66](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8772191/); [Rossetti LC, et al. Int J Mol Sci. 2011;12(10):7271-7285](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3211038/); [Pandelache A, et al. Genes (Basel). 2021;12(6):798](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8225079/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 18 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 presents type 3 dysbetalipoproteinemia as an 'equal cholesterol and TG elevation' with tuberoeruptive xanthomas, which reads as a pattern you can recognise from a standard panel. Heidemann 2022: FD is usually a mixed hyperlipidemia but can present as predominant hypertriglyceridemia or hypercholesterolemia; the standard lipid panel cannot detect the phenotype (ultracentrifugation VLDL-C/total TG >0.30 by mass, or apoB-based methods, are needed, plus a causal APOE genotype for a formal diagnosis); about 90% of cases are e2e2, but only 10-15% of e2e2 people express the phenotype (Pallazola 2020 gives fewer than 5%); the broad-beta band is highly specific but has low sensitivity. Pallazola 2020: HLP3 cannot be diagnosed from the standard lipid profile. Sampson 2022: striate palmar xanthomas are considered pathognomonic, while tubero-eruptive xanthomas also occur in other dyslipidemias. _(evidence: [Heidemann BE, Koopal C, Baass A, et al. Clin Genet. 2022;102(4):253-261](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9543580/); [Sampson M, Wolska A, Meeusen JW, et al. Front Genet. 2022;13:935257](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9329831/); [Pallazola VA, Sathiyakumar V, Park J, et al. Arch Med Sci. 2020;16(5):993-1003](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7444722/))_

Kumari 2021 lists loss of function in LPL, apoC-II, apoA-V, LMF1 and GPIHBP1 as causes of FCS. _(evidence: [Wolska A, Dunbar RL, Freeman LA, et al. CDC DPDx states molecular methods are currently recommended for distinguishing pathogenic Entamoeba species and that conventional PCR is the method of choice in reference laboratories. Only some antigen kits are histolytica-specific (several detect E. histolytica antigen but do not exclude E. dispar), and antigen utility is limited for frozen, fixed or post-treatment specimens. _(evidence: [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 31 · High-Yield Hits #11 — The reviewer says an ESR >100 mm/h in a known cancer patient usually means metastases. Abbag 2007 says that in adults, solid tumours causing an ESR >=100 mm/h are often (not usually) associated with metastasis. It also reports infection as the most common cause of ESR >=100 in paediatric and adult series, with malignancy third. Sensitivity of ESR >=100 for malignancy is low: 0.25 in the 1006-patient series summarised by Markanday 2015 and 25-49% per Abbag 2007. Markanday also reports specificity of 0.96 for malignancy and an identifiable cause in 90%. So an extreme ESR in a cancer patient, especially one with fever, needs a search for infection and cannot be read as metastasis by default. _(evidence: [Abbag FI, Al Qahtani JM. Ann Saudi Med. 2007;27(3):175-178](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6077077/); [Markanday A. Open Forum Infect Dis. 2015;2(3):ofv098](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4525013/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 31 · High-Yield Hits #11 — The reviewer says temporal arteritis/polymyalgia rheumatica typically run >90 mm/h. No open source read supports a typical >90 mm/h value. Manzo 2018 reports that 7-22% of PMR patients have a normal ESR at diagnosis, usually with a raised CRP, and that biopsy-proven GCA can occur with a normal ESR and CRP. In Kermani 2012 (abstract read; full text not open access), an elevated ESR had a sensitivity of 84.1% for a positive temporal artery biopsy, and 4% of biopsy-positive GCA patients had both ESR and CRP normal. _(evidence: [Manzo C, Milchert M. Reumatologia. 2018;56(1):1-2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5911650/); [Kermani TA, et al. Semin Arthritis Rheum. 2012;41(6):866-871](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3307891/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 27 · Exam Traps #4 — Trap 4 says functional tests (erythrocyte Cu-Zn superoxide dismutase activity) are more meaningful than serum copper. The one study read was in rats. In it, erythrocyte and liver SOD1 activity did not fall significantly in mild zinc-induced copper deficiency (they fell only on a frankly copper-deficient diet), while copper chaperone for SOD1 (CCS) did fall. The authors note that no gold standard exists. The trap's point that serum copper and ceruloplasmin are confounded and thought to miss marginal deficiency is supported. _(evidence: [Iskandar M, et al. Nutr J. 2005;4:35](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1315358/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 27 · Exam Traps #6 — Trap 6 says plasma is preferred, and the Number Vault trick says serum runs 5-10% higher than plasma. The BOND zinc review reports that early studies found serum zinc slightly higher than plasma, put this down to serum samples standing longer before separation, and found no difference when both were held equally long. BOND's suggested lower plasma zinc cut-offs also differ by fasting state, time of day and sex (µg/dL): morning fasting 70 F / 74 M; morning non-fasting 66 F / 70 M; afternoon 59 F / 61 M. The trap's points on diurnal variation and haemolysis (blood cells hold about 10 times plasma zinc) are supported. _(evidence: [King JC, et al. J Nutr. 2015;146(4):858S-885S](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4807640/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 27 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 gives low serum copper (RBC copper normal) for Menkes syndrome. Tümer 2010 says reduced serum copper and ceruloplasmin support the diagnosis but should be interpreted with caution in newborns, because healthy newborns also have low levels. It describes the plasma DOPA:DHPG catecholamine ratio as a possible rapid neonatal test and ATP7A molecular testing as the ultimate proof. The sources read did not mention 'RBC copper normal'. _(evidence: [Tümer Z, Møller LB. Eur J Hum Genet. 2010;18(5):511-518](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2987322/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and the Number Vault) give factor V Leiden as 5-7% of whites, ~7x heterozygous risk and 'lifetime penetrance only ~10%'. Nakashima & Rogers 2014 give heterozygous prevalence ~3-5% in Caucasians and 4-8x heterozygous / 80x homozygous venous risk; Khan & Dickerman 2006 give 3-7% and 7x / 80x; the BSH 2022 guideline gives ~5x for a first venous thrombosis. No source read states a ~10% lifetime penetrance. Khan & Dickerman cite an estimate that homozygotes can be expected to have at least one VTE in their lifetime, and a family study in which at least 25% of carriers had a thrombosis by age 50, so the figure may understate risk, especially for homozygotes. _(evidence: [Nakashima MO, Rogers HJ. Blood Res. 2014;49(2):85-94](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4090343/); [Khan S, Dickerman JD. Thromb J. 2006;4:15](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1592479/); [Arachchillage DJ, et al. Br J Haematol. 2022;198(3):443-458](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9542828/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 72 · Exam Traps #3 — Trap 3 (and the Number Vault) call the MTHFR 677C>T thrombotic association 'questionable' and the test 'largely dropped'. Deloughery 2022 (with the World Thrombosis Day Steering Committee) states MTHFR polymorphisms are not risk factors for venous or arterial thrombosis and should not be in any thrombophilia panel; the ASH 2023 guideline describes them as shown not to be associated with VTE; BSH 2022 does not recommend testing for MTHFR variants (Grade 2C). The reviewer's advice (test factor V Leiden and prothrombin 20210A instead) stands, but the wording understates the evidence. _(evidence: [Deloughery TG, et al. Res Pract Thromb Haemost. 2022;6(4):e12739](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9175241/); [Middeldorp S, et al. Blood Adv. 2023;7(22):7101-7138](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10709681/); [Arachchillage DJ, et al. Br J Haematol. 2022;198(3):443-458](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9542828/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #46 — Hit 46 says 16S rRNA sequencing 'gives a definitive bacterial ID'. Janda 2007 reports genus-level identification in most cases (>90%) but species-level identification less often (65-83%), poor discrimination for some groups (two Bacillus type strains >99.5% similar; Enterobacter/Pantoea; Streptococcus mitis group), and warns that the closest database match cannot always be taken as a definitive identification. Church 2020 states that the 16S target alone may not reliably identify many human pathogens and cannot differentiate species within the M. tuberculosis complex. 16S is an appropriate next step for an unidentifiable isolate, but the result is not always definitive at species level. _(evidence: [Janda JM, Abbott SL. J Clin Microbiol. 2007;45(9):2761-2764](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2045242/); [Church DL, et al. Clin Microbiol Rev. 2020;33(4):e00053-19](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7484979/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #45 — Hit 45 says MALDI-TOF 'does NOT do susceptibility testing'. This is true of the routine identification spectrum, but Calderaro 2024 describes MALDI-based beta-lactamase, carbapenemase and cephalosporinase activity assays (MBT STAR kits) and resistance-marker methods, used to guide early therapy before AST results are available. Phenotypic AST is still required. _(evidence: [Calderaro A, Chezzi C. Microorganisms. 2024;12(2):322](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10892259/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #42 — The reviewer gives a fixed list of microbiology critical values (positive CSF Gram stain, positive blood cultures, positive AFB smear, positive malaria smear) and a single read-back procedure. Malaria parasites are listed under haematology in the Croatian recommendations and the Portuguese survey. Read-back is recommended but practised inconsistently. The listed items are reasonable examples, not a universal list. _(evidence: [Lenicek Krleza J, et al. What is Case Surveillance (NNDSS)](https://www.cdc.gov/nndss/what-is-case-surveillance/index.html))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 (and Exam Trap 5) says SDS-PAGE separates proteins by molecular mass 'alone'. Estimating MW this way assumes every protein binds a consistent amount of SDS. Full-length membrane proteins show gel shifts of about -46% to +48% from their formula MW, and in model membrane hairpins the shifts tracked altered SDS loading. Highly acidic proteins can appear larger than their predicted MW, and glycosylation can slow migration. Mass-based separation is still the correct principle, but 'alone' needs this caveat. _(evidence: [Rath A, et al. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A. 2009;106(6):1760-1765](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2644111/); [Guan Y, et al. Sci Rep. 2015;5:13370](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4550835/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #17 — Hit 17's 'large molecules elute first' is correct as the ideal rule. But SEC separates by size in solution (Stokes radius), which does not track molecular weight exactly because proteins differ in shape. Interactions with the packing can also shift elution: ion exclusion makes a protein elute earlier than predicted, and ionic adsorption or hydrophobic interaction makes it elute later. _(evidence: [Hong P, et al. J Liq Chromatogr Relat Technol. 2012;35(20):2923-2950](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3556795/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 47 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 says the MS workup uses 'IFE' on unconcentrated CSF paired with serum, without naming isoelectric focusing. Elsewhere in Ch. 47 (Hits 8, 12, 13, 16) 'IFE' means immunofixation electrophoresis, while some literature (Jin 2023) uses 'IFE' for isoelectric focusing electrophoresis, so the acronym is ambiguous. The open sources describe the reference method as isoelectric focusing (IEF) followed by IgG-specific immunoblotting or immunofixation, with CSF and serum run in parallel at equal IgG. Trbojevic-Cepe 2004 states that high-resolution zone electrophoresis with immunofixation is inferior to IEF with immunofixation for detecting CSF-restricted bands. A student could read Hit 11 as describing serum-style immunofixation electrophoresis. _(evidence: [Trbojevic-Cepe M. EJIFCC. 2004;15(3):86-94](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6034201/); [Jin H, Lu Q, Gao F, Hao H. Ann Transl Med. 2023;11(7):282](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10116428/); [Hegen H, et al. PLoS One. 2019;14(4):e0215410](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6464233/); [Rammohan KW. Ann Indian Acad Neurol. 2009;12(4):246-253](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2824952/))_

- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and Exam Trap 1) explains the 60-70% PCR detection of t(14;18) as 30-40% of breakpoints falling outside the MBR. The open sources give the MBR as 50-65% and the mcr as 10-20% of breakpoints. They attribute the 60-70% PCR sensitivity to primers covering only short DNA segments, and say it rises to about 88% with multiple primer sets. They give FISH close to 100%. Detection therefore depends on how many breakpoint regions the primer sets cover. _(evidence: [Panjan M, et al. Radiol Oncol. 2023;57(4):487-492](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10690741/); [Selvi N, et al. Turk J Haematol. 2012;29(2):126-134](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3986950/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 gives a binary IGHV cut-off (unmutated <=2% divergence) and says high-risk CLL needs BTK/BCL2 inhibitors. ERIC confirms the 98% identity cut-off but adds two points. Cases at 97-97.99% identity are borderline and should be interpreted with caution. Stereotyped subset #2 is adverse whatever its mutation status. Longer BTK-inhibitor follow-up also showed no PFS difference between mutated and unmutated CLL. _(evidence: [Agathangelidis A, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(8):1961-1968](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9343247/); [Chatzikonstantinou T, et al. Leukemia. 2024;38(3):679-680](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10912022/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 gives MYD88 L265P in >90% of LPL/WM. Western series report MYD88 mutation (almost always L265P) in about 93-97%, but a large Chinese cohort found 87.8%. Detection depends strongly on method: Sanger 61.4%, NGS 74.4%, AS-PCR 84.2%, ddPCR 79.0%. MYD88 L265P also occurs in IgM MGUS (up to 87%) and in about 30% of ABC-type DLBCL, so it is not an exclusive diagnostic marker. _(evidence: [Yan Y, et al. Clin Cancer Res. 2024;30(23):5483-5493](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11609624/); [Shin SY, et al. Blood Res. 2016;51(3):181-186](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5054250/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 8 · Exam Traps #4 — Trap 4 says the most likely cause of a wild potassium delta-check swing is preanalytic mislabelling. The open sources list other common causes too: a real clinical change or treatment, IV-fluid contamination, clots, and haemolysis, lipaemia or icterus. One CBC study found transfusion was the commonest cause of haemoglobin and platelet delta failures (60.1%), with preanalytical errors at 6.3%. The recommended workup reviews clinical data first and checks the analyser last. _(evidence: [Lenicek Krleza J, et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2019;29(2):020502](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6559616/); [Kim MS, et al. Ann Lab Med. 2023;43(5):418-424](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10151276/))_

The 4.0–6.0% figure is laboratory- and method-dependent and was not verified in any open source. _(evidence: [American Diabetes Association Professional Practice Committee for Diabetes. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 53 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 presents anti-HMGCR as the marker of statin-associated necrotizing myopathy. Statin exposure is common but not required: a sizable minority (the majority in some cohorts) of anti-HMGCR myopathy patients, generally younger, never took statins (Palterer 2018: statin history relevant mainly in patients older than 50); the antibody is absent in self-limited statin myopathy. _(evidence: [Mohassel P, Mammen AL. J Neuromuscul Dis. 2018;5(1):11-20](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5836404/); [Palterer B, et al. Clin Mol Allergy. 2018;16:5](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5840827/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 53 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 states anti-Ro52/TRIM21 with anti-ARS means more severe myositis and joint impairment plus increased cancer and ILD risk. The ILD association is well supported, but Palterer 2018 states the role of anti-Ro52 in muscle severity has not been identified. Yamaguchi 2024 found more frequent and more severe ILD, Raynaud phenomenon and sicca symptoms in anti-Jo-1 patients, and cites earlier studies linking anti-Ro52 to myositis activity (one study) and to mechanic's hands and malignancy. Neither source supports more joint impairment. _(evidence: [Palterer B, et al. Clin Mol Allergy. 2018;16:5](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5840827/); [Yamaguchi K, et al.
