- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 53 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 gives anti-NT5c1A a specificity above 90% for sIBM. Specificity depends on assay, cutoff and comparator group. Amlani 2019 found 91.8% by ALBIA, with no positives in its RA, primary Sjogren and JDM groups, and reports earlier sensitivities of 33-80%. Palterer 2018 cites other reports of anti-cN-1A in about 30% of Sjogren syndrome, 20% of SLE and 12% of healthy children. _(evidence: [Amlani A, et al. Front Immunol. 2019;10:745](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6465553/); [Palterer B, et al. Clin Mol Allergy. 2018;16:5](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5840827/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 23 · High-Yield Hits #13 — Hit 13 presents fecal elastase-1 200 μg/g as the cut-off that detects pancreatic insufficiency. The 2025 European PEI guideline (Statement 2.7) says a cut-off for PEI cannot be established, although 200 μg/g is frequently used. It adds that the test is at risk of false positives at low and false negatives at high pretest probability, and that formed stool should be used whenever possible. UK practical guidelines read <200 μg/g as suggesting moderate and <100 μg/g as suggesting severe PEI, and note that watery samples are diluted and give false-positive (falsely low) results. A 2025 meta-analysis found pooled specificity of only 0.69 at 200 μg/g. _(evidence: [Dominguez-Muñoz JE, et al. United European Gastroenterol J. 2025;13(1):125-172](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11866322/); [Phillips ME, et al. BMJ Open Gastroenterol. 2021;8(1):e000643](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8212181/); [de la Iglesia D, et al. United European Gastroenterol J. 2025;13(8):1571-1582](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12529004/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 23 · Exam Traps #6 — Trap 6 (and Hit 14) state that ~2% of coeliac patients have selective IgA deficiency and advise IgG-based serology (DGP-IgG). ESsCD 2025 gives 2–3%, defines total IgA deficiency as serum IgA <7 mg/dL, and recommends IgG anti-TG2 or IgG anti-DGP. It adds that, because IgG tests are less sensitive, a negative IgG result does not exclude coeliac disease, and that patients with signs of malabsorption should have duodenal biopsy regardless of IgG serology. _(evidence: [Al-Toma A, et al. United European Gastroenterol J. 2025;13(10):1855-1886](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12704582/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 21 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 calls macroenzymes enzyme-autoantibody complexes with an IgG-like half-life of about 3 weeks. Open sources describe high-molecular-weight complexes of an enzyme with immunoglobulins or lipoproteins, or self-polymers. Macroamylase is typically IgA (about 70%) and less often IgG (about 30%). The ~3-week half-life was not found in the sources read. _(evidence: [Dedeene L, et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2023;33(1):010705](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9807236/); [Čásenská J, et al. - **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 21 · Exam Traps #2 — Trap 2 (and the matching Memory Hook) says citrate plasma gives falsely low CK because chelators strip calcium, the CK cofactor. A review describing the IFCC reference CK method states the reaction mixture contains EDTA to bind Ca2+ ions, so the reference method does not treat calcium as a required CK cofactor. The chelator effect on CK may run through another ion; this needs checking against the IFCC method text, which could not be read. _(evidence: [Radišić Biljak V, et al. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2025;35(2):020502](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12161516/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 78 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 and the Do Not Confuse table say 1p/19q codeletion defines oligodendroglioma. WHO CNS5 (2021) and EANO 2021 define oligodendroglioma as IDH-mutant AND 1p/19q-codeleted (CNS WHO grade 2-3). Ball 2020 adds that only whole-arm codeletion qualifies and that FISH cannot distinguish partial from whole-arm loss, giving an estimated 3.6% (8/223) false-positive rate in adult diffuse astrocytic gliomas. _(evidence: [Louis DN, et al. Neuro Oncol. 2021;23(8):1231-1251](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8328013/); [Weller M, et al. Nat Rev Clin Oncol. 2021;18(3):170-186](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7904519/); [Ball MK, et al. Neurooncol Adv. 2020;2(1):vdaa109](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7654379/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 78 · Exam Traps #4 — Hit 2, Exam Trap 4 and the Do Not Confuse table present 1p/19q codeletion as the predictor of PCV response. RTOG 9402 long-term results support doubled median survival with PCV plus RT in codeleted tumours (14.7 vs 7.3 years) and no difference in non-codeleted tumours (2.6 vs 2.7 years). That result comes from an unplanned analysis, and the authors say the codeletion was 'not flawlessly predictive'. EANO 2021 also recommends radiotherapy followed by PCV for IDH-mutant astrocytoma grade 2 (RTOG 9802), so PCV is not exclusive to codeleted tumours. _(evidence: [Cairncross G, et al. J Clin Oncol. 2013;31(3):337-343](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3732012/); [Weller M, et al. Nat Rev Clin Oncol. 2021;18(3):170-186](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7904519/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 13 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4, Exam Trap 1, the Do-not-confuse table (Table 2) and its closing line call up-coding and unbundling 'illegal fraud' with no qualification. The OIG laboratory compliance guidance says intentional or knowing upcoding 'could violate the False Claims Act and/or other civil laws, and criminal law'. The False Claims Act makes a person liable for knowingly presenting a false or fraudulent claim. It defines 'knowingly' to include deliberate ignorance and reckless disregard, and requires no proof of specific intent to defraud. The CMS NCCI manual treats unbundling and upcoding as improper coding that providers must avoid, and says NCCI was developed to prevent inappropriate payment. In these sources, fraud liability depends on knowing conduct; they do not treat every coding error as fraud. _(evidence: [Office of Inspector General, HHS. Publication of OIG Compliance Program Guidance for Clinical Laboratories. Fed Regist. 1998;63(163):45076-45087](https://oig.hhs.gov/documents/compliance-guidance/806/cpglab.pdf); [31 U.S.C. 3729 (False claims)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/31/3729); [CMS. Medicare NCCI Policy Manual, Chapter I, revision 1/1/2026](https://www.cms.gov/medicare/coding-billing/national-correct-coding-initiative-ncci-edits/medicare-ncci-policy-manual))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 13 · Exam Traps #6 — Trap 6 (and Hit 9) say the lab must give the patient an ABN before the test if Medicare may deny it. That is right as far as it goes, but it leaves out conditions in the CMS ABN form instructions and the OIG laboratory guidance. CMS: deliver the ABN before the service, far enough in advance for an informed choice; review it with the patient; give a reason for each listed test; keep a signed copy; ABNs are never required in emergencies. OIG: the notice must be written, identify the particular service, state that payment is likely to be denied and give the reason. Patients should not be asked to sign blank ABNs. Routine notices that only say denial is possible, or notices given for all services, are not acceptable. The OIG also notes that labs often find it hard to obtain ABNs directly and may educate ordering physicians on their use. _(evidence: [CMS. Form Instructions, Advance Beneficiary Notice of Non-coverage (ABN), OMB 0938-0566](https://www.cms.gov/medicare/medicare-general-information/bni/downloads/abn-form-instructions.pdf); [Office of Inspector General, HHS. Publication of OIG Compliance Program Guidance for Clinical Laboratories. Fed Regist. 1998;63(163):45076-45087](https://oig.hhs.gov/documents/compliance-guidance/806/cpglab.pdf))_
- **needs-verification** · contradiction · Ch. 55 · High-Yield Hits #13 — The reviewer says C4d is prominent in 90% of immune-complex GN but negative/trace in 90% of C3 glomerulopathy, calling it a clean discriminator. In 41 C3 glomerulopathy biopsies (27 DDD, 14 C3GN) stained for C4d by immunohistochemistry, Singh 2019 found C4d negative/trace in only 22% of dense deposit disease and 64% of C3GN. The authors concluded that glomerular C4d should not exclude C3 glomerulopathy. A negative C4d supports an alternative-pathway process, but C4d does not reliably discriminate. _(evidence: [Singh G, Singh SK, Nalwa A, et al. Kidney Int Rep. 2019;4(5):698-709](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6506704/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 55 · Exam Traps #3 — The reviewer presents PLA2R epitope spreading (CysR to CTLD1/CTLD7) as a predictor of poorer remission without noting that epitope-specific anti-PLA2R assays are not available in clinical practice. Clinicians rely instead on the correlation of epitope spreading with total anti-PLA2R titres. _(evidence: [Tesar V, Hruskova Z. Front Immunol. 2021;12:593288](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8019786/); [Akiyama S, Imai E, Maruyama S. F1000Res. 2019;8:F1000 Faculty Rev-734](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6537914/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 55 · Exam Traps #3 — The reviewer's trap ('A single anti-PLA2R level tells you the MN prognosis') implies that one level carries no prognostic information. Tesar 2021 reports that baseline titre is prognostic: spontaneous remission is much less frequent with high titres, and high pre-treatment levels predict a worse treatment response. Serial titres are still needed to monitor immunologic remission and relapse. _(evidence: [Tesar V, Hruskova Z. Front Immunol. 2021;12:593288](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8019786/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 67 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 (and the Number Vault) presents ~1 SNP per 1000 bp as a fixed genome rate. Bell 2002 gives this as an approximation, but resequencing of 313 genes across many individuals found on average about one SNP every 185 bp (studies range from ~1 per 52 bp to ~1 per 500 bp by gene/region; Hoehe 2004), and a typical genome differs from the reference at 4.1-5.0 million sites, >99.9% of variants being SNPs and short indels (1000 Genomes 2015). The density depends on whether two copies, one genome vs the reference, or a population is compared. _(evidence: [Bell JI. Arthritis Res. 2002;4(Suppl 3):S273-S278](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3240131/); [Hoehe MR, Kroslak T. Dialogues Clin Neurosci. 2004;6(1):5-26](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3181790/); [1000 Genomes Project Consortium, Auton A, et al. Nature. 2015;526(7571):68-74](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4750478/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 67 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Hit 14 says CFTR mutation analysis expanded cystic fibrosis to milder/atypical forms (CBAVD, chronic pancreatitis, sinopulmonary disease). The Cystic Fibrosis Foundation consensus labels partial or single-organ phenotypes in people with CFTR mutations who do not meet CF diagnostic criteria CFTR-related disorders (some may later meet CF criteria), states that CF cannot be diagnosed simply by the presence of two CFTR mutations, and keeps the sweat chloride test as the primary confirmatory test. The reviewer wording can be read as calling all these patients CF. _(evidence: [Farrell PM, Rosenstein BJ, White TB, et al. J Pediatr. 2008;153(2):S4-S14 (read via a page-fetch tool)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2810958/); [Esposito MV, Aveta A, Comegna M, et al. J Clin Med. 2020;9(12):3853](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7760778/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 52 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 says XLA patients must NEVER receive live vaccines. The CDC/ACIP Altered Immunocompetence table for severe antibody deficiencies (e.g. XLA, CVID) lists specific contraindicated live vaccines (OPV, smallpox, LAIV, BCG, Ty21a, yellow fever, MMR, MMRV, dengue) and does not list varicella or rotavirus; the narrative says persons with impaired humoral immunity may receive varicella vaccine, notes IGIV interferes with the response to measles and possibly varicella vaccine, and advises that patients with quantitative B-cell deficiencies on immunoglobulin therapy should not receive live or non-live vaccines during that therapy. The blanket rule therefore largely holds in practice for a treated XLA patient but is broader than the listed contraindications. US guidance only; Philippine rules are not covered. _(evidence: [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Altered Immunocompetence. General Best Practice Guidelines for Immunization (ACIP). Page dated June 26, 2024 (read via a page-fetch tool)](https://www.cdc.gov/vaccines/hcp/acip-recs/general-recs/immunocompetence.html))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 52 · High-Yield Hits #13 — Hit 13 (and the Memory Hooks, Number Vault and Exam Trap 2) give a fixed 5-6-month IVIG hold before evaluating vaccine responses. No source read states that interval. The AAAAI/ACAAI/JCAAI practice parameter, as summarised by Perez 2017, lets young specific-antibody-deficiency patients who have stabilised on IgG stop treatment for 4-6 months and then be re-evaluated; Hansen 2021 excluded antibody measurements within 6 months of IgG replacement. The interval appears to be source- and context-dependent rather than a single rule. _(evidence: [Perez E, et al. Front Immunol. 2017;8:586](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5439175/); [Hansen AT, et al. Front Immunol. 2021;12:717873](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8514775/))_

- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 46 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 gives the stimulation index as cpm(stimulated)/cpm(control) as if universal. One open source defines SI as the ratio of mean stimulated to mean unstimulated response, but another laboratory's methods define it as (stimulated − unstimulated cpm)/unstimulated cpm × 100; that laboratory's normal PHA SI range was very wide (15–267), and for its <10%-of-normal threshold parallel healthy controls were more reliable than previously established references. Formula and cutoffs are laboratory-defined. _(evidence: [Lee HJ, Gau CC, Lee WF, et al. Front Pediatr. 2022;10:638549](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9082031/); [Ganesan N, Ronsmans S, Hoet P. Heliyon. 2023;9(9):e19242](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10471999/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 45 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 says the antibody-bound FPIA tracer forms a '>160 kDa complex'. Maragos 2009 describes intact IgG as about 150 kDa binding a tracer under 1 kDa (about 151 kDa together), and no source read gives 160 kDa. Low impact: the principle (small free tracer depolarizes, bound tracer is polarized) is correct. _(evidence: [Maragos C. Toxins (Basel). 2009;1(2):196-207](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3202780/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 45 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 gives rare-earth chelate decay as '~50–100 µs' against background '<10 ns'. Hagan 2011 gives lanthanide luminescence as microseconds to milliseconds (example BCPDA–Eu chelate 0.44–0.76 ms) and background fluorescence as nanosecond-scale. The reviewer's figure is narrower than the range the source gives and sits below its example chelate lifetimes. _(evidence: [Hagan AK, Zuchner T. Anal Bioanal Chem. 2011;400(9):2847-2864](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3102841/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 44 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 states the normal CD4:CD8 ratio as a fixed 2:1 and explains the HIV change only as CD4 loss. McBride and Striker 2017 say the normal ratio in healthy people is poorly defined: ratios of 1.5-2.5 are generally considered normal, and sex, age, ethnicity, genetics, exposures and infections may all affect it. In untreated HIV, circulating CD8 cells typically rise before CD4 cells fall, and on ART some patients keep high CD8 counts and a low ratio. The hit correctly gives CD4 ~60% as a share of CD3+ cells, but reports usually give percentages of all lymphocytes: in Choi 2014 the healthy adult means were CD4 40.6% and CD8 25.2% of lymphocytes (ratio 1.81), so comparing a per-lymphocyte value with 60% misreads a normal panel. _(evidence: [McBride JA, Striker R. PLoS Pathog. 2017;13(11):e1006624](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5667733/); [Choi J, et al. Immune Netw. 2014;14(6):289-295](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4275386/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 28 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 says high O2 gives falsely low POCT glucose but does not name the strip chemistry. Oxygen competes with the mediator on glucose oxidase strips (high O2 low, low O2 high). Glucose dehydrogenase strips are less sensitive to oxygen. _(evidence: [Erbach M, et al. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2016;10(5):1161-1168](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5032951/); [Ginsberg BH. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2009;3(4):903-913](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2769960/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 28 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 says acetaminophen causes markedly false-positive continuous glucose monitor readings. The effect depends on the system. Acetaminophen can cause erroneously high readings in certain electrochemical meters and real-time CGMs, but a newer CGM with a permselective membrane showed a mean interference of 3.1 mg/dL after a 1 g dose, below the 10 mg/dL clinically meaningful threshold. That study was manufacturer-affiliated. _(evidence: [Calhoun P, et al. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2018;12(2):393-396](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5851234/); [Erbach M, et al. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2016;10(5):1161-1168](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5032951/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 28 · Exam Traps #4 — Hit 9 and Trap 4 explain the haematocrit effect as RBCs occluding the strip and anaemia letting extra plasma through. The sources read support the direction of error: low haematocrit (<35%) tends to read too high and high haematocrit reads too low. They describe several mechanisms, including red cell glucose content and cells blocking the electrode or enzyme. They do not state the plasma-flow explanation. Haematocrit-correcting meters are less susceptible. _(evidence: [Ginsberg BH. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2009;3(4):903-913](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2769960/); [Erbach M, et al. J Diabetes Sci Technol. 2016;10(5):1161-1168](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5032951/))_
- **needs-verification** · outdated · Ch. 9 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Gives a warfarin INR target of 3–4 for valve replacements. Open sources describe 2.0–3.0 as the most common mechanical-valve range and 2.5–3.5 for a mechanical mitral valve or an added risk factor (as used in the RE-ALIGN trial protocol); in a 671-patient mechanical-valve cohort with ranges set from guideline recommendations and risk factors, only 1 patient had a 3.0–4.0 target. The reviewer's own Ch. 39 Number Vault uses 2.5–3.5 for mechanical mitral valves. The ESC/EACTS guideline table could not be read directly, so this needs verification. _(evidence: [Langenaeken T, et al. J Clin Med. 2023;12(15):4984](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10419922/); [Johansson I, et al. Thromb Haemost. 2024;124(7):613-624](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11199045/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 9 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Says D-dimer 'confirms' DIC and lists decreased fibrinogen as part of the picture. Guideline summaries state that no single marker can diagnose DIC (FDP/D-dimer also rise in venous thromboembolism and after surgery), that DIC is diagnosed with a scoring system (ISTH overt DIC total >=5 is compatible with overt DIC), and that reduced fibrinogen is not observed in most septic DIC. _(evidence: [Wada H, et al. J Intensive Care. 2014;2(1):15](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4267589/); [Iba T, et al. J Intensive Care. 2023;11(1):24](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10202753/); [Toh CH, et al. Ann Lab Med. 2016;36(6):505-512](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5011102/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 9 · Exam Traps #4 — Hit 8 and Trap 4 read a mixing study from correction alone (correction = deficiency; no correction = inhibitor). Santoro 2023 adds that anticoagulant contamination of the sample must be excluded before mixing, and that factor VIII autoantibodies in acquired haemophilia A are usually time-dependent: the immediate mix may correct, and 2 h incubation at 37 C is needed to show the inhibitor. _(evidence: [Santoro RC, et al. Medicina (Kaunas). 2023;59(6):1169](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10305031/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 74 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 (also the Memory Hook '100 = 99:1, just subtract one', the Number Vault, the Calculation Check worked example and 5-Second Recall) says PI >= 100 means odds of 99:1 in favor of paternity. Under Bayes' rule, posterior odds = prior odds x PI. With a 0.5 prior (1:1 odds) a PI of 100 gives odds of 100:1, and the probability of paternity is PI/(PI+1) = 100/101, about 99.0%. '99:1' confuses odds with the percentage. _(evidence: [Thompson WC, Vuille J, Biedermann A, Taroni F. Front Genet. 2013;4:220](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3809556/); [Thelingwani RS, Jonhera CA, Masimirembwa C. Sci Rep. 2024;14(1):1385](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10791675/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 74 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 says AABB Standards (2019) require PI >= 100 for parentage determination. The Standards text is not openly available and was not read. The open source read reports that, per the 2013 AABB relationship-testing annual report, 65% of accredited laboratories used an LR threshold of 100 for standard trios; it says thresholds vary among laboratories and that other thresholds (such as 10 and 1,000) may be used depending on the relationship. China's parentage specification uses CPI >= 10,000. This is US/AABB practice; Philippine parentage-testing rules are not covered. _(evidence: [Ge J, Budowle B. PLoS Genet. 2020;16(8):e1008929](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7425842/); [Gao H, Wang C, Zhang R, et al. PLoS One. 2019;14(11):e0225174](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6853303/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 74 · High-Yield Hits #13 — Hit 13 and Exam Trap 4 say indirect (reverse-homozygosity) exclusions in >=2 independent systems are generally sufficient for nonpaternity. Sources read say putative fathers are not excluded on one or two STR mismatches because these may be mutations (consensus: more than two mismatches usually excludes). One laboratory excluded only with >=4 excluding loci and CPI < 10,000. With modern STR panels a two-locus rule is not clearly supported; the AABB Standards text was not read. _(evidence: [De Kock AA, Kloppers JJ. Afr Health Sci. 2021;21(1):379-384](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8356597/); [Gao H, Wang C, Zhang R, et al. PLoS One. 2019;14(11):e0225174](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6853303/); [Thelingwani RS, Jonhera CA, Masimirembwa C. Sci Rep. 2024;14(1):1385](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10791675/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 71 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 gives Turner karyotypes as 45,X 50%, 46,X,i(Xq) 20%, X deletions/rings or Y material 15%, mosaics 10%. The 2024 international guideline (Table 1) gives 45,X 40-50%, 45,X/46,XX 15-25%, 46,X,i(Xq)/idic(Xp) ~15%, 45,X/46,XY 10-12%, 45,X/47,XXX mosaics ~3%, ring X and unbalanced X-autosome translocations rare. Mosaics are much more frequent than 10%, and i(Xq) is about 15%, not 20%. The Number Vault row repeats the same figures. _(evidence: [Gravholt CH, Andersen NH, Christin-Maitre S, et al. Eur J Endocrinol. 2024;190(6):G53-G151](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11759048/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 71 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 says any Y material means gonadoblastoma risk; the imported simulator explanation goes further and says it 'warrants gonadectomy'. The 2024 guideline reports gonadoblastoma rates from 0% to 100% across studies, recommends PCR screening for Y material in 45,X individuals with signs of virilisation and states that searching for Y material in all 45,X individuals is not endorsed, and recommends individualised decision-making about gonadectomy and its timing, weighing gonadoblastoma/dysgerminoma risk against gonadal function and fertility (R 7.40). _(evidence: [Gravholt CH, Andersen NH, Christin-Maitre S, et al. Eur J Endocrinol. 2024;190(6):G53-G151](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11759048/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 71 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 says a 21;21 Robertsonian carrier has 100% Down syndrome offspring or nonviable conceptions. Zhao 2015 agrees that homologous carriers make only disomic or nullisomic gametes, which give trisomic or monosomic conceptuses, but notes that a phenotypically normal child can occasionally result from postzygotic trisomy rescue. Zhao also notes that most homologous rearrangements are likely isochromosomes and that most homologous ROBs (including der(15;15)) involve uniparental disomy. _(evidence: [Zhao WW, Wu M, Chen F, et al. PLoS One. 2015;10(5):e0122647](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4416705/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 70 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 (and the Number Vault) gives DASL as profiling up to 1536 targets from degraded RNA. April et al. 2009 say this limit applied to the original DASL assay. They extended it to a whole-genome DASL that covers all 24K well-annotated RefSeq genes and characterized it on FFPE tissue, so the 1536 figure on its own leaves out that the assay was later extended. _(evidence: [April C, Klotzle B, Royce T, et al. PLoS One. 2009;4(12):e8162](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2780295/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 59 · High-Yield Hits #2 — The reviewer's Mantoux cutoff lists leave out several groups in CDC guidance. At 5 mm or more, CDC also lists organ transplant recipients and other immunosuppressed people (prolonged corticosteroids of 15 mg/day or more prednisone equivalent, TNF-alpha antagonists). At 10 mm or more, CDC also lists mycobacteriology laboratory workers, children under 5, people who live or work in high-risk congregate settings (CDC examples: nursing homes, homeless shelters, correctional facilities), silicosis, people under 90% of ideal body weight, and infants, children and adolescents exposed to high-risk adults. CDC words the reviewer's 'foreign-born' group as people born in countries where TB disease is common, and its 5 mm radiograph group as chest x-ray findings suggestive of previous TB. _(evidence: [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Clinical Testing Guidance for Tuberculosis: Tuberculin Skin Test. Last updated 2025 Jan 31](https://www.cdc.gov/tb/hcp/testing-diagnosis/tuberculin-skin-test.html))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 59 · Exam Traps #3 — Exam Trap 3 says M. kansasii, M. szulgai and M. marinum are the only NTM exceptions that carry ESAT-6/CFP-10. Hit 3 and the 'KSM trio' memory aid list the same three. Pai 2014 also lists M. flavescens. The point that BCG strains lack these antigens is correct. _(evidence: [Pai M, Denkinger CM, Kik SV, et al. Clin Microbiol Rev. 2014;27(1):3-20](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3910908/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 59 · High-Yield Hits #2, #13 — The TST cutoff groups and the statement that BCG is 'NOT recommended in the U.S.' are US (CDC/ATS/IDSA) guidance. CDC says BCG is not generally used in the US but may be considered for certain continually exposed children and, individually, for health care workers in high-MDR-TB settings. The 2011 BCG World Atlas lists the Philippines with a BCG-at-birth policy. Current Philippine TB-testing and BCG rules are not covered by these sources. _(evidence: [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Bacille Calmette-Guerin (BCG) Vaccine for Tuberculosis. Last updated 2025 Jan 31](https://www.cdc.gov/tb/hcp/vaccines/index.html); [Zwerling A, Behr MA, Verma A, Brewer TF, Menzies D, Pai M. PLoS Med. 2011;8(3):e1001012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3062527/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #39 — Hit 39 presents quantitative lower-respiratory cultures (BAL >=10^4, PSB >=10^3, endotracheal aspirate >=10^5-10^6 CFU/mL) as the way to separate pathogen from oral-flora contamination. The IDSA/ATS 2016 HAP/VAP guideline suggests noninvasive sampling with semiquantitative cultures to diagnose VAP (weak recommendation, low-quality evidence) and does not recommend routine invasive quantitative cultures. The aspirate cut-off also differs by source: 10^6 CFU/mL in the IDSA/ASM 2018 guide, 10^5 CFU/mL in the studies the 2016 guideline summarises. _(evidence: [Kalil AC, et al. Clin Infect Dis. 2016;63(5):e61-e111](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4981759/); [Miller JM, et al. Clin Infect Dis. 2018;67(6):e1-e94](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7108105/))_
- **verified-issue** · uncertain · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #36 — Hit 36 states without qualification that acridine orange is more sensitive than the Gram stain for low-burden specimens. Sharma 2020 found higher AO sensitivity in CSF (96.9% vs 78.1% against culture) and cites a detection limit of about 10^4 CFU/mL for AO vs about 10^5/mL for Gram stain, but notes that other studies reported equivalent or much lower AO sensitivity and no clear advantage for ocular specimens. The relative sensitivity depends on specimen type and study. _(evidence: [Sharma S, et al. BMC Res Notes. 2020;13(1):29](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6958790/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #38 — Hit 38 says broad-range 16S rRNA PCR can detect bacteria in sterile fluids when cultures are negative, without noting false positives. Yoo 2020 (312 normally sterile fluids over about 10 years) confirms added yield (6.1% culture alone vs 11.2% combined; the clinically relevant sequencing-only findings all came from antibiotic-pretreated patients) but found 8 of 16 sequencing-positive, culture-negative results inconsistent with the clinical context, suggesting contamination from DNA extraction kits, PCR reagents or samples; results need clinical interpretation. _(evidence: [Yoo IY, et al. Ann Lab Med. 2020;40(1):63-67](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6713660/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Hit 12 instructs treating all SPACE organisms (Serratia, Pseudomonas, Acinetobacter, Citrobacter, Enterobacter) as resistant to extended-spectrum cephalosporins except cefepime. The ARLG AmpC primer names Enterobacter spp. (and K. aerogenes) as the most problematic for AmpC induction, reports that derepressed Serratia marcescens, Morganella morganii and Providencia stuartii express about ten-fold less AmpC than derepressed Enterobacter cloacae or Citrobacter freundii, states that because AmpC production is less prominent for the other 'SPACE' organisms it is reasonable to base treatment decisions on in vitro susceptibility results, and classes Acinetobacter baumannii chromosomal resistance as noninducible. It lists P. aeruginosa as having inducible chromosomal ampC but limits its treatment discussion to Enterobacteriaceae. It also states that observational data support both cefepime (preferentially every 8 hours) and piperacillin-tazobactam as acceptable, that the question is largely unsettled, and that carbapenem superiority is unproven. _(evidence: [Tamma PD, Doi Y, Bonomo RA, Johnson JK, Simner PJ. Clin Infect Dis. 2019;69(8):1446-1455](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6763639/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #42 — Hit 42 says never to culture a surface swab of an open wound. CODIFI (400 patients with suspected infected diabetic foot ulcer, England) found that a Levine-technique wound swab, taken after cleansing and debridement, still reported at least one potential pathogen in 70.1% of patients versus 86.1% for tissue (curette or scalpel). Pathogen results differed in 58% of patients, with more pathogens and fewer contaminants from tissue. Tissue collection caused more ulcer pain (9.3% vs 1.3%) and bleeding (6.8% vs 1.5%). The preference for tissue is therefore method- and collection-technique dependent rather than absolute. No open source read supports the aspirate option or the site-by-site pathogen map in the same item. _(evidence: [Nelson A, Wright-Hughes A, Backhouse MR, Lipsky BA, Nixon J, Bhogal MS, Reynolds C, Brown S. BMJ Open. 2018;8(1):e019437](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5879729/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #26 — Hit 26 links a sneaker-sole puncture wound to Pseudomonas aeruginosa osteomyelitis alone. The paediatric acute haematogenous osteomyelitis review read here lists both Staphylococcus aureus and Pseudomonas aeruginosa for puncture wounds. No open source read in this session supports the sneaker-specific association, so the single-organism framing needs verification. _(evidence: [Thakolkaran N, Shetty AK. Ochsner J. 2019;19(2):116-122](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6584206/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 50 · High-Yield Hits #10 — Hit 10 frames unrelated HCT matching as '10 of 10' (A, B, C, DRB1, DQB1) and says HLA-A mismatches have the largest impact on GVHD and survival. The 2019 NMDP/CIBMTR guideline uses 8/8 high-resolution matching at HLA-A, -B, -C and -DRB1 as the core standard. It reports that any single mismatch at these loci was associated with worse overall survival, with no evidence that mismatch at any one locus was better tolerated. It states that DQB1 has not been shown in isolation to substantially affect survival, and it recommends DPB1 typing with matched or permissive T-cell-epitope mismatches. _(evidence: [Dehn J, et al. Blood. 2019;134(12):924-934](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6753623/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 50 · Exam Traps #3 — Trap 3 states HLA-DP was never defined serologically. DP specificities were historically defined by cellular methods (HLA-DPw). However, the 2026 WHO Nomenclature Committee report (from its 2023 meeting) names serologic specificities for DPA1 and DPB1 (DPA01, DPB01 and so on), defined in silico from residues determining epitopes and assessed against patient-serum reactivity with single-antigen beads. The trap's main lesson, that DP matters in HCT, stands. _(evidence: [Marsh SGE, et al. HLA. 2026;107(3):e70595](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12936402/); [Dehn J, et al. Blood. 2019;134(12):924-934](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6753623/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 39 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 (also Memory Hooks and the Number Vault, where it is attributed to a meta-analysis) states that a low 4T score means a 0-3% HIT probability. In the sources, 0-3 is the POINT range of the low-probability 4Ts category (total 0-8). The low category's probability is given as <5%, with a negative predictive value of about 99.8%, and HIT has been reported with a score of 3. ASH 2018 recommends against HIT laboratory testing and against empiric treatment with a low-probability score. _(evidence: [Patriarcheas V, et al. Cureus. 2020;12(3):e7385](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7179984/); [Marchetti M, et al. J Clin Med. 2021;10(4):683](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7916628/); [Cuker A, et al. Blood Adv. 2018;2(22):3360-3392](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6258919/))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 39 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 (and the Number Vault, 98.1/89.4%) gives PF4-ELISA sensitivity ~98% and specificity ~89% as fixed values and mentions an excess-heparin confirmatory step. Sources give EIA sensitivity >99% and specificity that depends on the clinical context and the assay (IgG-specific assays are more specific than polyspecific ones), with one review citing 40-70%. Inhibition by high heparin concentrations is documented as a specificity feature of washed-platelet functional assays. The exact 98.1/89.4 figures and any EIA confirmatory step were not verified. _(evidence: [Warkentin TE, Greinacher A. Semin Thromb Hemost. 2023;49(6):621-633](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10421650/); [Patriarcheas V, et al. Cureus. 2020;12(3):e7385](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7179984/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 39 · High-Yield Hits #2 — Hit 2 (also Memory Hooks and Exam Trap 4) states that fondaparinux does not cross-react in vivo. Sources say fondaparinux is immunogenic but only rarely reported as a cause of HIT, and ASH 2018 calls its risk negligible. ASH 2018 lists it only as a conditional, very-low-certainty option alongside argatroban, bivalirudin, danaparoid and DOACs, and one review notes it is not approved for HIT treatment. _(evidence: [Marchetti M, et al. J Clin Med. 2021;10(4):683](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7916628/); [Cuker A, et al. Blood Adv. 2018;2(22):3360-3392](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6258919/); [Patriarcheas V, et al. Cureus. 2020;12(3):e7385](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7179984/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 35 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Hit 14 states normal B cells express kappa OR lambda light chains, 'never both', and that light-chain restriction proves clonality. Pauza et al. (abstract) found normal human fetal marrow and spleen B cells co-expressing surface kappa and lambda at 2-3% of surface-Ig+ B cells and state this refutes the concept that single light-chain isotype expression is immutable. Guillory et al. describe hematogones with lambda light-chain restriction by flow but no clonal IG heavy or kappa rearrangement by PCR, so flow restriction does not always prove clonality. Restriction remains a valid population-level sign of a clone (reactive kappa:lambda about 1.5:1 per Gajzer & Fromm 2025), but 'never' and 'proves' are too absolute. _(evidence: [Pauza ME, Rehmann JA, LeBien TW. J Exp Med. 1993;178(1):139-149](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2191066/); [Guillory T, Li S, Bergsagel DJ, Weinzierl E, Bunting ST. Lab Med. 2016;47(2):163-170](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4832592/); [Gajzer DC, Fromm JR. Cancers (Basel). 2025;17(5):814](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11898643/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 35 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 says 'CLL/SLL needs >=5,000 monoclonal B cells/μL' and that below that a CLL-phenotype clone is MBL. WHO-HAEM5 requires no other features diagnostic of CLL/SLL, separates low-count MBL (<0.5 x 10^9/L) from CLL/SLL-type MBL (>=0.5 x 10^9/L), and states the upper limit as a total B-cell count <5 x 10^9/L. A count below 5,000/μL alone therefore does not exclude CLL/SLL. SLL in particular is diagnosed without the leukaemic count, on lymphadenopathy and/or splenomegaly with lymph node biopsy (Chiattone 2025), so the 5,000/μL requirement applies to CLL, not SLL. _(evidence: [Alaggio R, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(7):1720-1748](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9214472/); [Galigalidou C, et al. Front Oncol. 2021;11:769612](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8631769/); [Chiattone CS, et al. Hematol Transfus Cell Ther. 2025;47(2):103822](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12131026/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 34 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 (and the Number Vault 'MDS cytopenias' row) gives MDS cytopenia thresholds as Hb <10 g/dL, ANC <1.8 and platelets <100 x10^9/L. Both 2022 classifications define cytopenia as Hb <13 g/dL in males / <12 g/dL in females, ANC <1.8 x10^9/L and platelets <150 x10^9/L, harmonized across CCUS, MDS and MDS/MPN. _(evidence: [Khoury JD, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(7):1703-1719](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9252913/); [Arber DA, et al. Blood. 2022;140(11):1200-1228](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9479031/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 34 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 uses MDS-EB1/EB2 and 'ring sideroblasts >=15% (or >=5% with SF3B1)'. WHO 2022 renames these MDS-IB1 (5-9% BM or 2-4% PB) and MDS-IB2 (10-19% BM or 5-19% PB or Auer rods). It defines MDS with low blasts and SF3B1 mutation by the mutation, and >=15% ring sideroblasts may substitute for the mutation (the term 'MDS with low blasts and ring sideroblasts' is kept for SF3B1 wild-type cases). ICC 2022 keeps MDS-EB for 5-9% BM (2-9% PB), calls 10-19% blasts MDS/AML, and defines MDS-SF3B1 by a VAF >=10% without requiring ring sideroblasts. The marrow cutoffs are unchanged, but the names and the ring-sideroblast rule depend on the classification. _(evidence: [Khoury JD, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(7):1703-1719](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9252913/); [Arber DA, et al. Blood. 2022;140(11):1200-1228](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9479031/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 34 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 (and Exam Trap 3 and the Number Vault 'AML diagnostic blasts' row) says APL with t(15;17) and inv(16) are diagnostic 'regardless of blast count'. This is incomplete and depends on the classification. WHO 2022 removes the 20% requirement for most AML with defining genetic abnormalities (including PML::RARA and CBFB::MYH11), keeps 20% for BCR::ABL1 and CEBPA, and requires morphology to correlate with the genetics. ICC 2022 requires at least 10% blasts or blast equivalents for AML with recurrent genetic abnormalities, APL included, and at least 20% for BCR::ABL1. Ch. 35 is covered by rv-leukemia-blasts. _(evidence: [Khoury JD, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(7):1703-1719](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9252913/); [Arber DA, et al. Blood. 2022;140(11):1200-1228](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9479031/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 25 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 says iodine excess shuts down synthesis '(hypothyroidism)' and 'iodine deficiency → Jod-Basedow phenomenon (hyperthyroidism)'. Leung and Braverman 2014 describe Jod-Basedow as iodine-induced hyperthyroidism, which follows an iodine LOAD. Its risk factors are nontoxic or diffuse nodular goitre, latent Graves disease and long-standing iodine deficiency, so deficiency is a risk factor rather than the trigger. They also describe the Wolff–Chaikoff reduction in hormone synthesis as transient (about 24 h in rats), with escape through decreased sodium–iodide symporter expression. Hypothyroidism develops in susceptible patients who fail to escape, for example those with autoimmune thyroid disease or previously treated Graves disease. _(evidence: [Leung AM, Braverman LE. Nat Rev Endocrinol. 2014;10(3):136-142](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3976240/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 25 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 gives TRAb/TSI as positive in ~85% of Graves disease. The 2018 ETA guideline reports a pooled TRAb sensitivity of 97% and specificity of 98% and calls TRAb specific biomarkers for Graves disease. Lee and Pearce 2023 report 97.7% sensitivity and 99.5% specificity for current third-generation assays. The figure depends on the assay generation, and ~85% understates current assays. _(evidence: [Kahaly GJ, et al. Eur Thyroid J. 2018;7(4):167-186](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6140607/); [Lee SY, Pearce EN. JAMA. 2023;330(15):1472-1483](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10873132/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 25 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 presents TSH as the first-line thyroid test without its limits. Koulouri 2013 (Table 1) lists conditions in which TSH alone may mislead: recent treatment for thyrotoxicosis (TSH may stay suppressed), non-thyroidal illness, TSH assay interference, central hypothyroidism, TSH-secreting adenoma, resistance to thyroid hormone, and disorders of thyroid hormone transport or metabolism. The same paper notes that many laboratories now combine TSH with T4. The 2018 ETA guideline states that diagnostic accuracy improves when both TSH and free T4 are measured. _(evidence: [Koulouri O, et al. Best Pract Res Clin Endocrinol Metab. 2013;27(6):745-762](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3857600/); [Kahaly GJ, et al. Eur Thyroid J. 2018;7(4):167-186](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6140607/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 11 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 and Exam Trap 3 (also Memory Hook and Number Vault) set the PT investigation threshold at more than about 2.5 SDI, with a 0.6% chance of being a true inlier. Kristensen and Meijer 2017 cite ISO/IEC 17043:2010: |z| up to 2 is satisfactory, between 2 and 3 is questionable (a warning signal that should be investigated), and 3 or more is unsatisfactory (an action signal). KEQAS treats SDI above 3 as unacceptable. Coucke and Soumali 2017 give 4.6% and 0.27% beyond |z| of 2 and 3 under normality. 0.6% is the one-tailed normal tail beyond 2.5 SD; the two-sided figure is about 1.2%. Cut-offs depend on the scheme. The advice to investigate a passing result still holds, but the 2.5 threshold and the 0.6% figure are not a general standard. _(evidence: [Kristensen GB, Meijer P. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2017;27(1):49-62](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5382861/); [Coucke W, Soumali MR. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2017;27(1):37-48](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5382862/); [Kim S, et al. Ann Lab Med. 2021;41(2):230-239](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7591290/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 11 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 and Exam Trap 1 (also the Memory Hook and 5-Second Recall) say that after a reagent lot change you adjust the QC target and never the SD. Thompson and Chesher 2018 say that when patient data show no clinically significant difference, the new lot can still show IQC changes in the running mean or SD, and the IQC targets can safely be altered. ICSH 2024 says IQC values (targets and limits) should be assessed for new coagulation reagent lots. For a new lot of unassayed control material, ICSH accepts limits from either a recalculated mean plus or minus 2 SD or the mean plus or minus 2 times the previously determined SD. The sources read do not support an absolute ban on changing the SD; whether to keep the established SD looks like a laboratory policy choice. CLSI C24 was not read. _(evidence: [Thompson S, Chesher D. Clin Biochem Rev. 2018;39(2):51-60](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6223607/); [Gosselin RC, et al. Semin Thromb Hemost. 2024;50(8):1091-1102](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11469916/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 73 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 (and its 5-Second Recall) says ADH1B His to Arg at 48 is the gain of function. The reference ADH1B*1 allele has Arg48 and the fast ADH1B*2 allele has His48 (70- to 80-fold higher turnover than beta1), so the gain-of-function change is Arg48 to His48. ALDH2*2 is Glu504Lys in full-length numbering (487 in the mature enzyme), so the reviewer's 487 reflects mature-protein numbering. _(evidence: [Edenberg HJ. Alcohol Res Health. 2007;30(1):5-13](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3860432/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 73 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 says CYP2D6/CYP2C19 poor and ultrarapid metabolizers need dose adjustments for tricyclics and SSRIs. CPIC often recommends avoiding the drug and choosing an alternative: tricyclics in CYP2D6 ultrarapid or poor metabolizers, tertiary amines in CYP2C19 ultrarapid/rapid and poor metabolizers, citalopram/escitalopram in CYP2C19 ultrarapid metabolizers (and an alternative considered first in poor metabolizers), paroxetine and vortioxetine in CYP2D6 ultrarapid metabolizers. Dose changes are the fallback when the drug is still used. These recommendations are CPIC-specific; other guidelines may differ. _(evidence: [Hicks JK, et al. Clin Pharmacol Ther. 2017;102(1):37-44](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5478479/); [Bousman CA, et al. Clin Pharmacol Ther. 2023;114(1):51-68](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10564324/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 73 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 frames DGKH as lithium pharmacogenomics. DGKH (diacylglycerol kinase eta, a key protein in the lithium-sensitive phosphatidylinositol pathway) was identified in the first GWAS of bipolar disorder, a case-control study of disease risk rather than of lithium response. As of 2015, no positive lithium-response genetic finding had been replicated in at least two samples of 200 or more subjects. The claim that lithium is the only mood stabilizer thoroughly examined pharmacogenomically was not verified by any source read. _(evidence: [Baum AE, et al. Mol Psychiatry. 2008;13(2):197-207](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2527618/); [Alda M. Mol Psychiatry. 2015;20(6):661-670](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5125816/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 56 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and the Number Vault row) gives hyper-IgE (Job) syndrome IgE as 2,000-50,000 kU/L (1 kU/L = 1 IU/mL). Galletta 2024 reports serum IgE of 2,000-100,000 IU/mL in STAT3-HIES and notes it may fall with age. It also describes several genetic forms: autosomal-dominant STAT3 (Job syndrome), autosomal-recessive (most often DOCK8), and PGM3, CARD11 and ZNF431. IgE above 2,000 IU/mL counts as markedly elevated ('hyper IgE'). _(evidence: [Galletta F, Gambadauro A, Foti Randazzese S, et al. Life (Basel). 2024;14(10):1329](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11509725/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 56 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 says mast-cell PGD2 comes via COX-2. Krystel-Whittemore 2015 states that mast cells express both COX-1 and COX-2. Rittchen 2019 cites a study of isolated human lung mast cells in which PGD2 generation relied largely on COX-1 and hematopoietic PGD synthase, with COX-2 absent at baseline and after stimulation. Naming COX-2 alone is therefore an oversimplification. _(evidence: [Rittchen S, Heinemann A. Cells. 2019;8(6):619](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6628301/); [Krystel-Whittemore M, Dileepan KN, Wood JG. Front Immunol. 2015;6:620](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4701915/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 56 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 (and the Number Vault row 'Leukotriene potency vs. histamine, up to 1000x') groups LTC4, LTD4 and LTE4 as up to 1000x more potent bronchoconstrictors than histamine. Kanaoka & Boyce 2014 report that inhaled LTC4 and LTD4 caused bronchoconstriction at doses several log-fold lower than histamine. They describe LTE4 as a weaker bronchoconstrictor than LTC4 and LTD4, so the potency statement applies to LTC4 and LTD4, not to all three equally. _(evidence: [Kanaoka Y, Boyce JA. Allergy Asthma Immunol Res. 2014;6(4):288-295](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4077954/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 32 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and 5-Second Recall Q4) present Ehrlich's aldehyde (Watson-Schwartz) screening as the test for acute intermittent porphyria. The AGA 2023 expert review makes random urine PBG, ALA and creatinine, with ALA and PBG normalised to creatinine, the initial test. It notes that rapid qualitative Hoesch/Watson-Schwartz tests have seen limited clinical use. Aarsand 2025 warns against using a qualitative urine PBG screen in an emergency without following it with a quantitative, more sensitive assay. During attacks, ALA and PBG are at least 5-fold the upper limit of normal. _(evidence: [Wang B, Bonkovsky HL, Lim JK, Balwani M. Gastroenterology. 2023;164(3):484-491](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10335308/); [Aarsand AK, To-Figueras J, Whatley S, et al. Liver Int. 2025;45(3):e16012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11815605/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 32 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 says free erythrocyte protoporphyrin rises in lead poisoning and iron deficiency. The sources describe the rise as zinc-chelated protoporphyrin (zinc goes in when iron is unavailable). Metal-free protoporphyrin is what marks erythropoietic protoporphyria, and Aarsand 2025 notes that erythrocyte 'total protoporphyrin' assays may, despite their name, not include the metal-free form. _(evidence: [Aarsand AK, To-Figueras J, Whatley S, et al. Liver Int. 2025;45(3):e16012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11815605/); [Hennig G, Homann C, Teksan I, et al. Nat Commun. 2016;7:10776](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4757790/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 32 · Exam Traps #2 — Trap 2 says antibiotics and obstructive jaundice both give falsely low fecal urobilinogen. The obstruction half is supported: in classic dog studies, total bile obstruction made urobilin disappear from bile and stool, and urobilinogen formation needs bile pigment delivered to the gut. The bacterial mechanism is also supported: germ-free rats excrete no stercobilinogen, and BilR is a gut-microbial enzyme. However, no open source read here measured the effect of antibiotics on human fecal urobilinogen; Kipp 2025 only says antibiotic effects on bilirubin levels warrant consideration. _(evidence: [McMaster PD, Elman R. J Exp Med. 1925;41(4):513-534](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2130956/); [Su H, Yang S, Chen S, et al. Int J Mol Sci. 2024;25(16):8582](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11354725/); [Kipp ZA, Pauss SN, Martinez GJ, et al. Antioxidants (Basel). 2025;14(11):1326](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12649357/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Gives Down syndrome detection of 94–96% for the quadruple screen and ~98% at <0.5% false positive for integrated screening. ACMG 2009 gives the quad screen ~75% in women under 35 (at a 5% screen-positive rate) and >80% in women 35 or older, the triple screen ~65%, first-trimester combined screening (NT + PAPP-A + hCG) 79–90% at a 5% false-positive rate, serum integrated ~88% and integrated 93–96%. It gives no figure near 98% at <0.5% and says the combined first- and second-trimester rates come from mathematical modelling. _(evidence: [Driscoll DA, Gross SJ, Professional Practice Guidelines Committee. Genet Med. 2009;11(11):818-821](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3111043/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #6 — States 'Advanced maternal age = 35 years' without context. ACMG 2009 says that age 35 or older at delivery was historically the trigger for offering diagnostic testing. It also says maternal age 35 is no longer recommended as the cutoff for deciding who is offered invasive prenatal diagnostic testing and that maternal age alone is a poor screening criterion. All women, regardless of age, should be offered screening and the option of diagnostic testing. _(evidence: [Driscoll DA, Gross SJ, Professional Practice Guidelines Committee. Genet Med. 2009;11(11):818-821](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3111043/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #7 — States that maternal serum AFP 'peaks at 12–14 weeks, falls ~10%/week in the second trimester'. Kim 2023 puts the AFP peak in FETAL serum at about 9–13 weeks and says AFP measured in pregnancy keeps rising until about 32 weeks before falling to term. Głowska-Ciemny 2024 puts the fetal serum maximum at 10–13 weeks and says AFP appears in maternal serum in week 6 and then decreases until delivery. The open sources disagree on the maternal curve, and neither supports a 12–14-week maternal peak with a ~10%/week second-trimester fall. _(evidence: [Kim GJ, Seong JS, Oh JA. Obstet Gynecol Sci. 2023;66(1):1-10](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9849725/); [Głowska-Ciemny J, et al. J Clin Med. 2024;13(2):466](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10816536/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 26 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Presents maternal serum AFP as the screening test for open neural tube defects (also Exam Trap 4). Kim 2023 reports that some countries now recommend mid-trimester ultrasound instead of MSAFP. It cites ACOG Practice Bulletin 187 and the Canadian JOGC/SOGC guideline: the JOGC names second-trimester ultrasound the primary NTD screen and limits MSAFP to settings where ultrasound is not feasible or maternal BMI is >=35. One comparison found detection of 96% with ultrasound vs 75% with MSAFP. ACMG 2009 offers MSAFP and/or ultrasound at 15–20 weeks. Practice depends on the guideline and the country, and Philippine practice is not covered. _(evidence: [Kim GJ, Seong JS, Oh JA. Obstet Gynecol Sci. 2023;66(1):1-10](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9849725/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 47 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 says MGUS is present in ~3% of people aged 70 or older. Abeykoon 2022, citing the Olmsted County study (Kyle 2006), gives about 3% at 50 or older and about 5% at 70 or older (3.2%, 5.3% and 7.5% at 50, 70 and 85 or older). The reviewer has attached the 50-and-older figure to the 70-and-older age group. _(evidence: [Abeykoon JP, Tawfiq RK, Kumar S, Ansell SM. Fac Rev. 2022;11:34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9720897/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 47 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 gives one rule for all MGUS: SPEP every 6-12 months. Abeykoon 2022 shows that follow-up depends on the guideline and on risk. The IMWG suggests a recheck at 6 months, then every 2-3 years for stable low-risk MGUS, or yearly for higher risk. The European Myeloma Network rechecks at 6 months and then yearly, but advises no further follow-up for low-risk MGUS that is stable at 6 months. The UK Myeloma Forum/Nordic Myeloma Study Group suggests every 3-4 months in the first year, then every 6-12 months, for low-risk MGUS. The International Expert Consensus suggests every 4-6 months for 2 years, then every 6-24 months. The reviewer's interval matches only part of one guideline (UK/Nordic, after year 1) and is not the IMWG schedule. _(evidence: [Abeykoon JP, Tawfiq RK, Kumar S, Ansell SM. Fac Rev. 2022;11:34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9720897/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 47 · High-Yield Hits #3 — Hit 3 says M-spikes of IgG 2 g/dL or more and IgA 1 g/dL or more are suspicious for malignancy. None of the open sources read gives these thresholds. Abeykoon 2022 uses M-protein of 1.5 g/dL or more and a non-IgG isotype as Mayo MGUS progression risk factors, with 3 g/dL as the MGUS/smoldering fence. Lakshman 2018 and Mateos 2020 use M-protein above 2 g/dL as a risk factor within smoldering myeloma (the Mayo 2/20/20 model), not as a malignancy threshold for MGUS. The reviewer's thresholds need a primary source. _(evidence: [Abeykoon JP, Tawfiq RK, Kumar S, Ansell SM. Fac Rev. 2022;11:34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9720897/); [Lakshman A, Rajkumar SV, Buadi FK, et al. Blood Cancer J. 2018;8(6):59](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5997745/); [Mateos MV, Kumar S, Dimopoulos MA, et al. Blood Cancer J. 2020;10(10):102](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7567803/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 47 · Exam Traps #5, #6 — The MGUS/smoldering/myeloma table (Exam Trap 5) and Exam Trap 6 separate MGUS from smoldering myeloma only by the <10% marrow plasma cell fence. Under the 2014 IMWG criteria, MGUS also needs serum M-protein below 3 g/dL (Abeykoon 2022). Smoldering myeloma is met by M-protein of 3 g/dL or more, or urine M-protein of 500 mg/24 h or more, and/or 10% or more clonal plasma cells (Lakshman 2018; Mateos 2020). So a patient with under 10% plasma cells but M-protein of 3 g/dL or more has smoldering myeloma, not MGUS. Light-chain MGUS is also defined by an involved:uninvolved FLC ratio below 100. _(evidence: [Abeykoon JP, Tawfiq RK, Kumar S, Ansell SM. Fac Rev. 2022;11:34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9720897/); [Lakshman A, Rajkumar SV, Buadi FK, et al. Blood Cancer J. 2018;8(6):59](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5997745/); [Mateos MV, Kumar S, Dimopoulos MA, et al. Blood Cancer J. 2020;10(10):102](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7567803/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #13 — The reviewer gives fixed impedance size windows: platelets 2-20 fL, RBCs >36 fL, WBCs >35 fL after RBC lysis. These windows depend on the analyser. An open source documents 2-20 fL platelet pulses for one Beckman Coulter analyser (LH 780). The GFHC states that impedance analysers differ in the size range they use and that they fit curves to the platelet histogram. No open source read here supports the RBC >36 fL or WBC >35 fL thresholds. _(evidence: [Saran K, et al. J Family Med Prim Care. 2022;11(10):6236-6242](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9810959/); [Baccini V, et al. J Clin Med. 2020;9(3):808](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7141345/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #12 — The reviewer says ISE potentiometry follows the Nernst equation but leaves out two points. ISEs sense ion activity in the water phase of serum, and analysers are calibrated assuming about 93% water. Indirect ISE dilutes the sample first, so it reports a falsely low sodium when lipids or proteins are very high (pseudohyponatremia, the electrolyte exclusion effect). Direct ISE on undiluted sample is not affected. _(evidence: [Aziz F, et al. J Clin Med. 2023;12(12):4076](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10299669/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #16 — Hit 16 says the 'ACMG incidental-findings list covers ~25 diseases'. That is the original 2013 list: 57 genes in 24 condition rows, reported regardless of patient preference. ACMG now calls these secondary findings (variants actively sought in a defined list). The 2021 update allows patients to opt out and says the list is updated every year. The ACMG SF v3.3 list (2025) has 84 genes, counted from its Table 1. _(evidence: [Green RC, et al. Genet Med. 2013;15(7):565-574](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3727274/); [Miller DT, et al. Genet Med. 2021;23(8):1391-1398](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12175738/); [Lee K, et al. Genet Med. 2025;27(8):101454](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12318660/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #16 — Hit 16 and Exam Trap 6 say predictive testing of children '<18' for adult-onset disorders is discouraged because of 'no medical benefit in childhood, but genuine psychosocial harm'. ASHG 2015 does advise deferring such testing to adulthood unless a clinical intervention is appropriate in childhood. But it gives the child's chance to make their own decision as the reason and names no fixed age. It also states that the limited research to date has not found evidence of significant psychosocial harms in children. _(evidence: [Botkin JR, et al. Am J Hum Genet. 2015;97(1):6-21](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4570999/))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 (and Exam Trap 5) give the exome as ~1.5% of the genome with 20,000-30,000 variants per exome and a diagnostic yield of 25-50%. Clark 2018 describes exome sequencing as covering about two percent of the genome. It reports a pooled exome diagnostic utility of 0.36 (95% CI 0.33-0.40) in children with suspected genetic disease, which falls inside the reviewer's range. Stitziel 2011 reports typically 15,000-20,000 variants per exome, with the number depending on the exome target and ancestry. All of these figures depend on capture design, population and indication. _(evidence: [Clark MM, et al. NPJ Genom Med. 2018;3:16](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6037748/); [Stitziel NO, et al. Genome Biol. 2011;12(9):227](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3308043/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 72 · High-Yield Hits #16 — The guidance on secondary findings and on predictive testing of minors comes from US professional societies (ACMG, ASHG). Philippine rules and professional guidance on genomic secondary findings and on testing minors are not covered and were not checked. _(evidence: [Miller DT, et al. Genet Med. 2021;23(8):1391-1398](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12175738/); [Botkin JR, et al. Am J Hum Genet. 2015;97(1):6-21](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4570999/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 7 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Hit 12 presents CLSI POCT01-A2 as the POCT connectivity standard with a 'device interface' and an 'observation reporting interface'. The CLSI catalogue lists POCT01-A2 (published July 28, 2006) as archived, meaning it is no longer reviewed through the CLSI consensus process, although CLSI says it is technically valid. The catalogue page describes the standard only as specifying common communication interfaces and protocols between systems and devices. No open source read in this session confirms the two interface names, and the standard itself was not read. _(evidence: [Clinical and Laboratory Standards Institute. POCT01-A2: Point-of-Care Connectivity; Approved Standard, Second Edition. Catalogue page.](https://clsi.org/shop/standards/poct01/); [Erasmus R, Sahni S, El-Sharkawy R. EJIFCC. 2021;32(2):190-194](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8343042/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 7 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 says procedures are reviewed annually and signed by the CLIA director or designee. 42 CFR 493.1251(d) requires procedures and changes in procedures to be approved, signed and dated by the current laboratory director before use. Section 493.1251 does not mention an annual review or a designee. Annual review may be an accreditation-body requirement, which was not checked here. US-only: Philippine rules are not covered. _(evidence: [42 CFR 493.1251 Standard: Procedure manual (eCFR, version 2026-10-01)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-493/subpart-K/section-493.1251))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 6 · Exam Traps #2 — The trap splits the work cleanly: middleware does autoverification, load balancing and reruns, and process-control software does physical chores. Armbruster 2014 says 'LAS or middleware' includes autoverification and autoretrieval for repeat, reflex and dilution testing, lists rule-based accept/reject decisions, autoverification, repeats, dilutions and QC rules among process-control software functions, and describes modern middleware as a process control and management tool. Krasowski 2014 says autoverification is commonly performed in the LIS and/or middleware. The split is a simplification; the functions overlap and their placement varies by system. _(evidence: [Armbruster DA, Overcash DR, Reyes J. Clin Biochem Rev. 2014;35(3):143-153](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4204236/); [Krasowski MD, Davis SR, Drees D, et al. J Pathol Inform. 2014;5(1):13](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4023033/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 6 · High-Yield Hits #6 — The open vs closed reagent item leaves out two things. First, the laboratory must perform method validation for every assay it adapts to an open system, whereas closed-system assay applications are optimised and verified by the manufacturer (Armbruster 2014); in the US, a modified FDA-cleared system or one not subject to FDA clearance requires the lab to establish its performance specifications (42 CFR 493.1253(b)(2)). Second, 'open/closed' is also used for TLA track connectivity: open tracks connect heterogeneous instruments from many companies, closed tracks connect only specific devices, generally the same company's (Nam & Park 2025; Armbruster 2014). _(evidence: [Armbruster DA, Overcash DR, Reyes J. Clin Biochem Rev. 2014;35(3):143-153](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4204236/); [Nam Y, Park HD. Ann Lab Med. 2025;45(5):472-483](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12370808/); [42 CFR 493.1253 (eCFR, version 2026-10-01)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-493/subpart-K/section-493.1253))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #13 — Hit 13 says no homozygous factor B deficiency has been described because it is 'likely embryonic lethal'. Bougeard 2026 reviews the reported cases of complete hereditary factor B deficiency (the genetically confirmed ones are compound heterozygous for loss-of-function variants, including nonsense plus frameshift). It states that complete deficiency does not impair growth or development, and one patient was diagnosed in adulthood. So the lethality rationale is not supported. The narrower claim that no homozygous case exists was not checked. _(evidence: [Bougeard C, Eskander E, Martins PV, et al. Eur J Immunol. 2026;56(2):e70148](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12886615/))_
- **verified-issue** · uncertain · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and the Number Vault) says humans carry 2–4 C4 genes, exceptionally up to 6. Wang 2021 gives C4A copy numbers of 0–5 and C4B of 0–4, with a highest reported total of 7. Macedo 2016 gives a C4 copy-number range of 2–8, most commonly two of each isotype. The reviewer's upper bound is lower than these sources report. _(evidence: [Wang H, Liu M. Front Immunol. 2021;12:694928](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8317844/); [Macedo AC, Isaac L. Front Immunol. 2016;7:55](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4764694/))_
- **verified-issue** · uncertain · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #4 — Hit 4 gives C2 deficiency as 1 in 10,000 Caucasians, the most common complement protein deficiency, with 20–40% developing SLE-like disease. Macedo 2016 gives homozygous prevalence as 1:10,000–20,000 in Western Europeans, more than 60% asymptomatic, and SLE in about 10–30% (10% in its summary table). In the ESID series of 77 complement-deficient patients that Macedo cites, C2 deficiency was the most common deficiency observed (29%), which supports the reviewer's 'most common' wording for that series. The 28-bp deletion is confirmed (El Sissy 2019: exon 6 skipping, about 90% of complete C2 deficiency). _(evidence: [Macedo AC, Isaac L. Front Immunol. 2016;7:55](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4764694/); [El Sissy C, Rosain J, Vieira-Martins P, et al. Front Immunol. 2019;10:1936](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6694794/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 51 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 defines complotype only as the MHC complement haplotype (CFB, C2, C4A, C4B inherited together). Harris 2012 notes that this is the term's original meaning but uses complotype for inherited common variants across the whole complement system, such as CFH, C3 and CFB. The SC01 example and the ≥3-Mb extended haplotype were not verified. _(evidence: [Harris CL, Heurich M, Rodriguez de Cordoba S, Morgan BP. Trends Immunol. 2012;33(10):513-521](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3460238/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 29 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 equates struvite coffin-lid crystals plus urease-positive organisms with staghorn calculi. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 29 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 presents calcium oxalate crystals + metabolic acidosis + osmolal gap as the ethylene glycol pattern without noting that crystals are often absent early: in a six-patient series only 1 had urinary oxalate crystals on admission (oxalate crystalluria is described 24-72 h after ingestion), and calcium oxalate also occurs in normal urine. Lee 2022 describes elongated narrow-hexagon calcium oxalate monohydrate crystals as the form typically found in ethylene glycol poisoning. _(evidence: [Latus J, Kimmel M, Alscher MD, Braun N. Clin Kidney J. 2012;5(2):120-123](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4235595/); [Lee AJ, Yoo EH, Bae YC, Jung SB, Jeon CH.
