- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 29 · High-Yield Hits #16 — Hit 16 identifies budding ovals with pseudohyphae as Candida and says RBCs don't bud. Poloni 2020 warns that yeast and pseudohyphae in urine sediment may be sample contaminants (mainly with improper collection) and should not be overinterpreted, that abundant pseudohyphae appear when urine stands, and that yeast can resemble erythrocytes, acanthocytes, lipid droplets and calcium oxalate monohydrate. The sources read do not state budding as the separating feature. _(evidence: [Poloni JAT, Rotta LN. J Fungi (Basel). 2020;6(4):E245](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7711825/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 presents the lab-only MELD (3.8 ln bilirubin + 11.2 ln INR + 9.6 ln creatinine + 6.4) as the transplant-priority score. US allocation moved to MELD-Na (adds sodium) in 2016, and MELD 3.0 (adds sex and albumin; creatinine cap 3.0 mg/dL) was approved by UNOS in 2023. The published coefficients are 9.57/3.78/11.20/6.43, and the original 2000 model also carried a cause-of-cirrhosis term that was later dropped. Allocation rules are US OPTN/UNOS policy; Philippine rules are not covered. _(evidence: [Guo G, et al. J Clin Transl Hepatol. 2025;13(1):62-68](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11712091/); [Pinter M, et al. ESMO Open. 2016;1(2):e000042](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5070280/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 gives Child-Pugh class A/B/C 2-year survival of 85%/57%/35%. These figures were not found in the open sources read. Pinter 2016 gives 1-year survival of about 95%/80%/44%, and survival by class differs between cohorts. _(evidence: [Pinter M, et al. ESMO Open. 2016;1(2):e000042](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5070280/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Hit 12 says AFP >400 ng/mL is highly probable hepatocellular carcinoma. The AASLD 2023 guidance says AFP at 400 ng/mL was previously a diagnostic criterion, but AFP is no longer recommended for HCC diagnosis. Diagnosis rests on noninvasive imaging criteria (LI-RADS) or pathology, a new solid lesion of 1 cm or more goes to multiphase CT or contrast-enhanced MRI, and more than 40% of HCCs have a normal AFP. A meta-analysis gives a pooled sensitivity of only about 32% (specificity about 99%) at 400 ng/mL. _(evidence: [Singal AG, et al. Hepatology. 2023;78(6):1922-1965](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10663390/); [Zhang J, et al. PLoS One. 2020;15(2):e0228857](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7018038/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 22 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Hit 12 gives 20 ng/mL as the cirrhosis screening cutoff. AASLD 2023 describes a 20 ng/mL cut-off in surveillance as about 60% sensitive and 90% specific, and notes that the optimal cut-off may be lower in nonviral cirrhosis. The APASL 2017 guideline recommends 200 ng/mL rather than 20 ng/mL when AFP is combined with ultrasound, so the cut-off depends on the guideline. _(evidence: [Singal AG, et al. Hepatology. 2023;78(6):1922-1965](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10663390/); [Omata M, et al. Hepatol Int. 2017;11(4):317-370](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5491694/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 15 · High-Yield Hits #10 — Hit 10 and Exam Trap 3 give one correction factor (1.6 mEq/L per 100 mg/dL glucose) as if it were settled. Baldrighi 2018 reports no consensus. Katz (1973) gives 1.6, which ADA DKA/HHS guidance reports (as Baldrighi notes). Hillier (1999) gives 2.4, which the European hyponatremia guideline (as Baldrighi reports) and the Korean Society of Nephrology 2022 recommendations use. Hillier also found that even 2.4 underestimates the fall above 400 mg/dL, where a factor of about 4 is more reliable. _(evidence: [Baldrighi M, et al. Curr Diabetes Rev. 2018;14(6):534-541](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6237920/); [Lee Y, et al. Korean J Intern Med. 2022;37(6):1120-1137](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9666265/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 15 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 (and Exam Trap 4) says pseudohyperkalemia has NO physiologic consequence. That holds for the patient's potassium, but Asirvatham 2013 notes that the same preanalytical artifacts can push a truly low potassium into the reference interval (masked hypokalemia). Fujimaru 2025 notes that pseudohyperkalemia can lead to unnecessary treatment, including potassium restriction and binders. _(evidence: [Asirvatham JR, et al. N Am J Med Sci. 2013;5(4):255-259](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3662091/); [Fujimaru T, et al. Clin Exp Nephrol. 2025;29(11):1524-1534](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12568891/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 15 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8, Exam Trap 4 and the HIT-FLED memory hook (D = decreased-temperature cold agglutinins) list cold agglutinins but omit cold storage or transport temperature of the sample. The reviews read describe cold storage as a cause: it inhibits the Na/K pump and gives seasonal (winter) pseudohyperkalemia. Cold agglutinins were found only in a single case abstract (Mody 2026), so the checklist names a rare cause and misses a common one. _(evidence: [Asirvatham JR, et al. N Am J Med Sci. 2013;5(4):255-259](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3662091/); [Uminska A, et al. Diagnostics (Basel). 2026;16(16):2676](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13511837/); [Mody J, et al. Spartan Med Res J. 2026;11(2):165959](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13451105/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 10 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 (repeated in Exam Trap 2) says significance can 'always' be forced or manufactured by inflating n. Sullivan and Feinn 2012 state that with a sufficiently large sample a test will almost always show a significant difference unless the effect size is exactly zero, and Greenland 2016 notes that when the test hypothesis and all other assumptions are true, it is rejected (wrongly) only at the alpha rate over very many uses of the test. So a large n makes tiny non-zero effects significant; it does not manufacture significance for a true null. _(evidence: [Sullivan GM, Feinn R. J Grad Med Educ. 2012;4(3):279-282](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3444174/); [Greenland S, et al. Eur J Epidemiol. 2016;31(4):337-350](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4877414/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 10 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and Exam Trap 3) presents the Bonferroni correction as the remedy for multiple comparisons without caveats. Ranganathan 2016 says Bonferroni has been criticised as far too conservative, especially for highly correlated tests, that alpha adjustment raises Type II (false-negative) risk, and lists Holm, Hochberg and false-discovery-rate (Benjamini-Hochberg) procedures. Andrade 2019 says Bonferroni magnifies Type II risk, that the Hochberg procedure balances the two errors better, that correction mainly applies to many tests of the same construct, and that it may be dispensed with in exploratory studies. _(evidence: [Ranganathan P, Pramesh CS, Buyse M. Perspect Clin Res. 2016;7(2):106-107](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4840791/); [Andrade C. Indian J Psychol Med. 2019;41(1):99-100](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6337927/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 76 · High-Yield Hits #9 — The reviewer gives pancreatic K-ras ctDNA ~10% and CA 19-9 ~30% sensitivity, combining to >60% (~65% with HGF/OPN/CEA). In Cohen 2017, the study this panel comes from (221 resectable stage I–IIB PDAC), KRAS ctDNA was 30%, CA 19-9 49%, combined 60%, and the five-marker panel 64%, with 1 of 182 healthy controls positive. _(evidence: [Cohen JD, et al. Proc Natl Acad Sci U S A. 2017;114(38):10202-10207](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5617273/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 76 · High-Yield Hits #6 — The reviewer states 25–50% of stage III/IV breast cancers have serum HER2 40- to 190-fold above normal; this figure was not found in the sources read. A meta-analysis reports serum ECD >15 ng/mL in 20–80% of metastatic and 4–10% of early-stage breast cancers, and in 2,862 stage I–III primary cancers only 15% of tissue HER2-positive tumours had raised serum ECD, so serum ECD does not define HER2 status (tissue IHC/ISH does). _(evidence: [Wu Y, et al. Cancers (Basel). 2022;14(19):4551](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9559205/); [Lee SB, et al. BMC Cancer. 2014;14:929](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4295268/))_

- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 75 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 states 'CYP2D6*10 (Asians ~50%)'. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #27 — Reviewer: needlestick in a vaccinated worker = check anti-HBs; inadequate titer -> HBIG + booster. US guidance adds context: documented responders (anti-HBs >=10 mIU/mL after a complete series) need no action; documented nonresponders after two series get HBIG x2 one month apart if the source is HBsAg-positive or unknown; response-unknown workers are tested for anti-HBs at the same time as the source is tested for HBsAg, and anti-HBs <10 with a positive or unknown source means HBIG x1 plus starting revaccination (a full second series, not just one booster). With a negative source, they get one extra vaccine dose and are retested 1-2 months later. _(evidence: [Schillie S, et al. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 57 · High-Yield Hits #20 — Reviewer says recapping is prohibited. 29 CFR 1910.1030(d)(2)(vii) says contaminated needles must not be bent, recapped or removed unless the employer can show no alternative is feasible or a specific medical or dental procedure requires it. In that case only a mechanical device or a one-handed technique may be used. Shearing or breaking is prohibited outright. _(evidence: [29 CFR 1910.1030 Bloodborne pathogens (eCFR, version 2026-10-01)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-29/subtitle-B/chapter-XVII/part-1910/subpart-Z/section-1910.1030))_

- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #17 — Hit 17 equates low CSF glucose + high protein + neutrophilic pleocytosis with bacterial meningitis without noting that the pattern is often incomplete. In one series of 110 confirmed microbial meningitis cases (bacterial, mycobacterial, fungal or parasitic), hypoglycorrhachia (<40 mg/dL) was present in only 50.1%. CSF glucose had low sensitivity and moderate-to-high specificity, and neurosarcoidosis also lowered glucose. The white count may not be raised early in infection, and antibiotic-pretreated cases are more likely to have higher glucose and lower protein. A normal glucose therefore does not exclude bacterial meningitis. _(evidence: [Baud MO, et al. Neurol Neuroimmunol Neuroinflamm. 2018;5(1):e425](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5745359/); [Tacon CL, Flower O. Emerg Med Int. 2012;2012:320309](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3461291/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 66 · High-Yield Hits #37 — Hit 37 says VP shunt infections are most often coagulase-negative staphylococci and, in the same item, presents multiplex meningitis/encephalitis PCR panels as detecting bacterial, viral and fungal CSF targets. CoNS lead in many series (8 of 31 isolates, 25.8%, in a 2025 Saudi multicentre study), but gram-negative bacilli predominate at some centres (Acinetobacter 41%, Pseudomonas 16%, CoNS 13% in a 2015-2021 Pakistani series). The FilmArray ME panel targets 14 community-acquired agents and has no staphylococcal target. Its evaluators advise caution when interpreting results in patients with CNS hardware, particularly negative results, and state that the panel does not eliminate the need for culture. _(evidence: [Alqasmi M, et al. J Clin Med. 2025;14(6):2006](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11942643/); [Akram Asif A, et al. Antimicrob Resist Infect Control. 2023;12(1):75](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10408103/); [Leber AL, et al. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #21 — Hit 21 lists didymium glass (~600 nm, broad peak) next to holmium oxide (360 nm, sharp peak) as wavelength QA checks, but does not say why they differ. Reule 1976 judged didymium bands too wide, temperature-dependent and of variable rare-earth composition to be suitable for wavelength checking. Allen 2007 (NIST) lists 11 certified holmium oxide glass bands (241.5-637.5 nm; 360.8 nm is one of them) and says holmium filters must not be used to calibrate the transmittance (absorbance) scale, which uses neutral-density filters. _(evidence: [Reule AG. J Res Natl Bur Stand A Phys Chem. 1976;80A(4):609-624](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5293527/); [Allen DW. J Res Natl Inst Stand Technol. 2007;112(6):303-306](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4655923/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 4 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 gives ~90 V between dynodes and amplification up to ~10^6. Michail 2024 gives a potential difference of 100-200 V between dynodes and an overall gain of the order of 10^6; Zheng 2020 says the dynode cascade amplifies the signal more than a million times. The gain figure is roughly consistent, but 10^6 is not a ceiling. The ~90 V figure is not supported by the sources read. Both figures depend on tube design and operating voltage. _(evidence: [Michail C, et al. Sensors (Basel). 2024;24(19):6251](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11478476/); [Zheng W, et al. iScience. 2020;23(6):101145](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7243193/))_

The 2017 ASCP/CAP/AMP/ASCO guideline (recommendation 1) requires RAS mutational testing for patients considered for anti-EGFR therapy, covering KRAS and NRAS codons 12 and 13 (exon 2), 59 and 61 (exon 3) and 117 and 146 (exon 4) ('expanded' or 'extended' RAS). Testing KRAS alone, or exon 2 alone, is incomplete. _(evidence: [Sepulveda AR, et al. Am J Clin Pathol. 2017;147(3):221-260](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7263311/))_

- **verified-issue** · uncertain · Ch. 78 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 (and the Number Vault) gives BRAF mutation in 66% of melanomas (80% of those V600E) and BRAF V600E in about 45% of papillary thyroid cancers. Cheng 2018 gives BRAF mutation in about 40-60% of melanomas (about half of advanced melanomas), with V600E 84.6% of BRAF-mutant cases in its Table 1 (up to 90% in the text). The TCGA papillary thyroid study found BRAF mutations, mostly V600E, in 248/402 (61.7%) tumours. Crispo 2019 cites wide ranges (melanoma 30-70%, papillary thyroid 36-83%) that contain both reviewer figures. The reviewer's point estimates are therefore not outright wrong, but they differ from the main sources: 66% is above Cheng's 40-60%, and 45% is below the TCGA figure. Frequencies vary by cohort and assay. _(evidence: [Cheng L, et al. Mod Pathol. 2018;31(1):24-38](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5758899/); [Cancer Genome Atlas Research Network. Cell. 2014;159(3):676-690 (read via a page-fetch tool)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4243044/); [Crispo F, et al. Cancers (Basel). 2019;11(9):1388](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6770736/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 78 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 lists colorectal cancer (about 10%) alongside melanoma as BRAF V600E-targetable without qualification. In colorectal cancer, a BRAF inhibitor causes loss of ERK-to-EGFR negative feedback, so EGFR-mediated RAS activity reactivates MAPK signalling, and efficacy falls short of BRAF-inhibitor monotherapy in melanoma. The BEACON regimen combines encorafenib with cetuximab (with or without binimetinib) as a second- or third-line option (Kanat 2020). The 2017 ASCP/CAP/AMP/ASCO guideline recommends BRAF V600 testing for prognosis and for Lynch evaluation in MLH1-deficient tumours (Sepulveda 2017). The frequency (about 8% advanced, 14% stage II-III) is consistent with the reviewer. _(evidence: [Kanat O, et al. World J Gastrointest Oncol. 2020;12(10):1080-1090](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7579731/); [Sepulveda AR, et al. Am J Clin Pathol. 2017;147(3):221-260](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7263311/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 69 · High-Yield Hits #5 — The reviewer says TMA/NASBA need NO initial denaturation because they start from single-stranded RNA, so DNA contamination cannot inflate an RNA measurement. Obande 2020 describes an initial denaturation step for NASBA at a different temperature from the 41 °C reaction (65 °C for RNA, 95 °C for DNA), with the thermolabile enzymes added afterwards, and says NASBA cannot amplify dsDNA unless it is first denatured. Dunbar 2019 says precise temperature control in TMA, as in NASBA, matters for denaturing secondary structures, and lists a commercial TMA assay for hepatitis B virus, a DNA virus (Aptima HBV Quant Dx). What is missing in NASBA is DNA melting, not every heat step, and the blanket DNA-exclusion statement does not hold for every TMA assay. _(evidence: [Obande GA, Banga Singh KK. Infect Drug Resist. 2020;13:455-483](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7024801/); [Dunbar S, Das S. J Clin Virol. 2019;115:18-31](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7106405/))_
- **needs-verification** · method-dependent · Ch. 69 · High-Yield Hits #6 — The reviewer gives the third-generation bDNA HIV-1 RNA detection limit as 50 copies/mL. Elbeik 2000 reports a 50 copies/mL lower limit (dynamic range 50–500,000) for bDNA version 3.0, but Dunbar 2019 lists the dynamic range of the FDA-approved Versant HIV-1 RNA 3.0 assay as 75–500,000 copies/mL. Reported limits for the same assay generation differ between sources, and the package insert was not read. _(evidence: [Elbeik T, et al. J Clin Microbiol. 2000;38(3):1113-1120](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC86352/); [Dunbar S, Das S. J Clin Virol. 2019;115:18-31](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7106405/))_
- **needs-verification** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 69 · High-Yield Hits #7 — NEAR as the basis of the first CLIA-waived nucleic acid tests is a US regulatory fact. Loeffelholz 2020 describes ID NOW (previously Alere i) Influenza A & B as the first-ever CLIA-waived nucleic acid-based test, and Dunbar 2019 lists Alere i Influenza A & B and Alere i RSV as NEAR assays. Philippine rules for point-of-care molecular testing are not covered. The reviewer also lists group A strep among those first tests, which was not verified in the sources read. _(evidence: [Loeffelholz MJ, Tang YW. Emerg Microbes Infect. 2020;9(1):747-756](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7172701/); [Dunbar S, Das S. J Clin Virol. 2019;115:18-31](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7106405/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 64 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 says UL54 polymerase mutations mean high-level ganciclovir plus foscarnet/cidofovir cross-resistance, and that UL56 means letermovir resistance. The Fourth International CMV SOT guidelines say UL54 mutations usually emerge later, add ganciclovir resistance and likely cross-resist cidofovir and/or foscarnet, with the pattern depending on the mutation. High-level (20-30-fold) ganciclovir resistance can result from UL97 and UL54 mutations combined. Maribavir resistance maps to UL97 (most commonly T409M, H411Y). Letermovir resistance often involves UL56 codon 325, and low-grade letermovir mutations have also been found in UL51 and UL89. _(evidence: [Kotton CN, et al. Transplantation. 2025;109(7):1066-1110](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12180710/); [Alsanea MS, et al. Diagnostics (Basel). 2024;14(2):203](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10814084/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #15 — The reviewer cites CLSI M39-A4 (4th ed., 2014) for antibiogram rules. The current edition is M39 5th edition (2022). The core rules (diagnostic isolates only, final verified results, first isolate per species per patient per analysis period, at least 30 isolates, at least annual analysis) are unchanged. _(evidence: [Simner PJ, et al. J Clin Microbiol. 2022;60(10):e0221021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9580356/); [Tran C, et al. Microbiol Spectr. 2023;11(1):e0393922](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9927543/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 58 · Exam Traps #6 — The trap says that duplicate isolates inflate resistance percentages. One study supports this for MRSA (in one year at one university hospital, 39% with duplicates vs 37% without), and its authors note the pattern may not apply to other bacteria. The reviewer also leaves out that the first-isolate rule can itself underestimate resistance, because later resistant isolates from patients whose first isolate was susceptible are excluded. _(evidence: [Klinker KP, et al. Ther Adv Infect Dis. 2021;8:20499361211011373](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8111534/); [Horvat RT, et al. J Clin Microbiol. 2003;41(10):4611-4616](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC254318/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #11 — The reviewer attributes the requirement of at least 90% essential agreement (within one doubling dilution) for verifying a new AST method to CLSI M52. The open CLSI working group best-practice paper confirms EA and CA targets of at least 90% for evaluating AST methods against broth microdilution. M52 itself is not openly available, so its exact verification criteria were not checked. _(evidence: [Humphries RM, et al. J Clin Microbiol. 2018;56(4):e01934-17](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5869819/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 58 · High-Yield Hits #2 — The reviewer gives broth microdilution incubation as 35 °C in ambient air for 16–20 h. The open review that was read gives incubation of at least 16–24 h. CLSI M07 was not accessible, so the exact conditions could not be confirmed. _(evidence: [Gajic I, et al. Antibiotics (Basel). 2022;11(4):427](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9024665/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 54 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 and Exam Trap 4 (also the Number Vault table) say temporal artery biopsy has 'up to a 15% false-negative rate'. The EULAR imaging systematic review (Duftner 2018) says biopsy is highly specific but lacks sensitivity, with false negatives in up to 61% of patients compared with a clinical diagnosis of GCA. The rate depends on the reference standard and the population, so 15% understates the upper bound. The reviewer's core point (a negative biopsy does not exclude GCA) holds. _(evidence: [Duftner C, et al. RMD Open. 2018;4(1):e000612](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5845406/); [Maz M, et al. Arthritis Rheumatol. 2021;73(8):1349-1365](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12344528/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 54 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 and Exam Trap 4 say 4% of biopsy-confirmed GCA cases have BOTH ESR and CRP normal. In the source cohort (Kermani 2012, 177 biopsy-positive patients), the 4% (7 patients) had both normal without glucocorticoid use. 18 patients (10.2%) had both normal when the 11 already on glucocorticoids at testing are included. The figure is correct for untreated patients, but the reviewer leaves out that qualifier. _(evidence: [Kermani TA, et al. Semin Arthritis Rheum. 2012;41(6):866-871](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3307891/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 54 · High-Yield Hits #15 — Hit 15 separates Takayasu arteritis (onset <50) from GCA (>50). The 2022 ACR/EULAR Takayasu classification criteria use age <=60 at diagnosis plus imaging evidence of vasculitis as absolute entry requirements. The 1990 ACR criteria used onset <=40. Neither uses <50. Typical Takayasu onset is 10-40 years, and the GCA entry rule of >=50 matches the reviewer. Age cut-offs are classification rules, not diagnostic tests. _(evidence: [Hwang J, et al. J Rheum Dis. 2026;33(3):152-168](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13318816/); [Tecer D, et al. Sarcoidosis Vasc Diffuse Lung Dis. 2025;42(3):17094](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12536845/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 48 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 says quiescent SLE has a normal C4. Ayano 2023 reports that two patterns are both often seen: normal complement during active disease, and persistently low complement in remission (serologically active clinically quiescent SLE). It also says a persistently low C4 on its own may reflect a low C4 gene copy number rather than disease activity. So a normal C4 neither defines quiescence nor rules out activity. _(evidence: [Ayano M, Horiuchi T. Biomolecules. 2023;13(2):367](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9953581/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 48 · High-Yield Hits #12 — Hit 12 (and Exam Trap 2) uses factor B as a firm discriminator: normal factor B means classical activation, low factor B means alternative activation. The C3 glomerulopathy consensus lists reduced factor B among markers of alternative-pathway activation, but notes these abnormalities may vary over the disease course, and its Table 1 uses low C3 with normal C4 as the indicator. Ekdahl 2018 reports that factor B remains more or less unchanged in C3NeF-driven C3 glomerulopathy, and its schematic Figure 7 shows factor B consumed together with C3 and C4 during SLE exacerbations. C4 is the more reliable discriminator; factor B is a supportive but inconsistent clue. _(evidence: [Pickering MC, et al. Kidney Int. 2013;84(6):1079-1089](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3842953/); [Ekdahl KN, et al. Front Immunol. 2018;9:2237](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6207586/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 31 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 says Perls grades marrow storage iron 0–5+ with normal adult 2+, as if this were a single standard scale. The conventional Gale scale, as tabulated in Phiri 2009, runs from 0 (none) to 6 (very heavy). That study counted grades 0–1 as deficient stores and fragment grade 2 or more as positive fragment iron, and it graded only smears with at least seven fragments. Lahtiharju 2025 reports a lack of standardised methodology for interpretation and classified iron as none, low, normal or abundant. No open source read gives a 0–5+ scale with 2+ as normal. Hit 8 also says the stain shows hemosiderin and ferritin, but Sonoda 2025 states that iron from ferritin is too scarce to be detected; the stain detects haemosiderin. _(evidence: [Phiri KS, et al. J Clin Pathol. 2009;62(8):685-689](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2709917/); [Lahtiharju T, et al. Blood Adv. 2025;9(7):1608-1617](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11986213/); [Sonoda A, et al. Forensic Sci Int Synerg. 2025;11:100627](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12275683/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 31 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 says leukoerythroblastosis points to marrow space occupation (solid tumours/lymphomas and myeloproliferative disorders). It describes the picture as a frequent but not invariable presenting feature of myelofibrosis. The reactive causes are left out. _(evidence: [Langabeer SE. Hematol Transfus Cell Ther. 2025;47(1):103735](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11849063/); [Chew S, Kamangar M. Clin Med (Lond). 2024;24(5):100235](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11399640/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 7 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and the matching memory hook and flashcard) says Roche LIAT (2015) was the first CLIA-waived molecular test. Deak and Marlowe (2017) state that the first CLIA-waived molecular POC test to receive FDA approval was the Alere i Influenza A&B, in 2015. Gonzalez and McElvania (2018) list Alere i as isothermal amplification and the cobas Liat assays (influenza A/B and RSV, Strep A) as CLIA-waived real-time PCR, but do not say which came first. Only one source read makes the 'first' statement explicitly; an FDA press release could not be retrieved (404). _(evidence: [Deak E, Marlowe EM. Clin Microbiol Newsl. 2017;39(15):115-123](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7132510/); [Gonzalez MD, McElvania E. Infect Dis Clin North Am. 2018;32(1):19-34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7135313/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 7 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 and Exam Trap 4 say molecular Group A Strep POCT is sensitive enough that culture backup of negatives is no longer needed. Sources qualify this: a review says these tests 'may not require reflex to culture'; the 2012 IDSA guideline (back up a negative RADT with culture in children and adolescents) makes no recommendation on nucleic acid amplification tests; and a 2023 comparison of guidelines states that the role of POC NAAT is not discussed in current guidelines. The decision therefore depends on the assay instructions and local policy. Molecular tests also detect colonisation and dead organisms (not a test of cure) and group A assays miss groups C and G unless they target them. _(evidence: [Gonzalez MD, McElvania E. Infect Dis Clin North Am. 2018;32(1):19-34](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7135313/); [Shulman ST, et al. Clin Infect Dis. 2012;55(10):e86-102](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7108032/); [Pellegrino R, et al. Eur J Pediatr. 2023;182(12):5259-5273](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10746578/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 28 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 (and Exam Trap 3) state that hemolysis precludes iron (RBC iron; hemoglobin competes with ferrozine). Thomas 2002 reports the additive iron effect of hemolysis as insignificant because iron-porphyrin binding is stronger than iron-transferrin binding and iron methods measure only transferrin-released iron, while Ho 2021 found iron falsely increased at hemolysis index 3+ to 4+ on Abbott Alinity. The effect is method- and degree-dependent, not a universal exclusion; the ferrozine-competition mechanism was not found in an open source. _(evidence: [Thomas L. EJIFCC. 2002;13(4):95-98](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6208064/); [Ho CKM, et al. Pract Lab Med. 2021;25:e00232](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8145753/))_
- **verified-issue** · method-dependent · Ch. 28 · High-Yield Hits #5 — Hit 5 says lipemia scatters light giving falsely high end-points. Nikolac 2014 states the direction and size of lipemia interference in spectrophotometric methods depend on the wavelength, the direction of the indicator reaction and blanking, and differ between manufacturers (e.g., bilirubin strongly positive on one platform, strongly negative on another); Ho 2021 found the direction of Intralipid interference on direct bilirubin depended on analyte concentration. 'Falsely high' is not a general rule. _(evidence: [Nikolac N. Biochem Med (Zagreb). 2014;24(1):57-67](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3936974/); [Ho CKM, et al. Pract Lab Med. 2021;25:e00232](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8145753/))_

The reviewer figures may come from a specific package insert but are longer than open recommendations. _(evidence: [Savy N, et al. Hepat Med. 2018;10:105-115](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6140721/); [Bélanger-Quintana A, et al. Nutrients. 2022;14(13):2755](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9269083/))_
- **verified-issue** · guideline-dependent · Ch. 27 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 and the Number Vault present a vitamin D target of about 30 ng/mL as a single standard. Guideline bodies differ: Amrein 2020 names >20 ng/mL (50 nmol/L) as the primary treatment goal and says most authors consider <30 ng/mL (75 nmol/L) deficiency; Pilz 2019 reports that the 2011 Endocrine Society guideline recommends treating deficiency to at least 75 nmol/L, whereas the IOM report does not conclude there is added benefit of 75 over 50 nmol/L. Philippine guidance was not reviewed. _(evidence: [Amrein K, et al. Eur J Clin Nutr. 2020;74(11):1498-1513](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7091696/); [Pilz S, et al. Endocr Connect. 2019;8(2):R27-R43](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6365669/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 27 · High-Yield Hits #6 — Hit 6 gives vitamin D toxicity as >500 nmol/L, and the Number Vault gives about 200 ng/mL. The sources read put intoxication hypercalcaemia usually above 375 nmol/L (150 ng/mL), and the IOM classifies a sustained level above 125 nmol/L as potentially harmful. The reviewer's figure is higher than the source thresholds and gives no context. _(evidence: [Pilz S, et al. - **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 19 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 (and the memory hook '100 to confirm') says BNP 100 pg/mL diagnoses heart failure in acute dyspnoea. The ESC values summarised by Castiglione 2022 treat BNP < 100 ng/L as making acute HF unlikely, 100-400 ng/L as a grey zone and > 400 ng/L as likely. Higher values support HF but need further investigation to confirm it. In Breathing Not Properly, BNP 100 was 90% sensitive but only 76% specific. The reviewer's NPV 89%/PPV 79% figures were not verified. _(evidence: [Castiglione V, et al. Heart Fail Rev. 2022;27(2):625-643](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8898236/))_
- **needs-verification** · missing-context · Ch. 19 · High-Yield Hits #10 — Hit 10, Exam Trap 3 and the memory hook ('NT-proBNP is the only honest marker') say BNP must not be used on sacubitril/valsartan. Castiglione 2022 supports NT-proBNP as the biomarker of choice. Nishikimi 2022, however, reports that the BNP rise is modest (PARADIGM-HF: median BNP +19%, NT-proBNP -28% at 8-10 weeks) because neprilysin has low substrate specificity for BNP, and argues that BNP remains usable in the ARNI era. 'Only honest marker' overstates the point. _(evidence: [Castiglione V, et al. Heart Fail Rev. 2022;27(2):625-643](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8898236/); [Nishikimi T, Nakagawa Y. Biology (Basel). 2022;11(7):1034](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9312360/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 79 · High-Yield Hits #7 — Hit 7 (and Exam Trap 3) names overfitting as the SELDI-TOF-MS pattern-diagnostics pitfall. Open sources add that discriminating peaks can come from non-biologic experimental bias, such as sample handling, storage and run differences. A re-analysis of a public ovarian SELDI dataset found the best-separating m/z values below 500, in a region often treated as noise, and concluded there was a non-biologic experimental bias between the cancer and control samples. Peak amplitudes may decline with repeated freeze-thaw cycles, and instrument drift can shift peak positions. A later study with stringent protocols and an independent validation cohort reached 73-81% accuracy for ovarian cancer versus benign tumours and judged SELDI-TOF-MS not applicable to routine patient care. _(evidence: [Sorace JM, Zhan M. BMC Bioinformatics. 2003;4:24](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC165662/); [Wegdam W, et al. Proteome Sci. 2012;10(1):45](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3494530/); [Grizzle WE, et al. Cancer Inform. 2005;1:86-97](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657646/))_
- **verified-issue** · contradiction · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #10 — States JAK2, CALR and MPL together account for >95% of Philadelphia-negative MPN (and MPL 3–5% of ET). Luque Paz 2023 reports that about 10% of MPN patients are triple-negative (no JAK2, CALR or MPL mutation), with MPL in about 5–7% of ET and 7–10% of PMF. A negative driver panel therefore does not exclude ET or PMF. _(evidence: [Luque Paz D, et al. Blood. 2023;141(16):1909-1921](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10646774/))_
- **verified-issue** · outdated · Ch. 77 · High-Yield Hits #12 — States that an SF3B1 mutation lowers the MDS-RS ring-sideroblast threshold from 15% to 5%. That is the WHO 2016 rule (as described by Malcovati 2020). WHO 2022 defines MDS with low blasts and SF3B1 mutation by the mutation itself (blasts <5% BM and <2% PB; no del(5q), monosomy 7 or complex karyotype), with >=15% ring sideroblasts only as a substitute when no SF3B1 mutation is found; ICC 2022 classifies SF3B1-mutated MDS without excess blasts as MDS-SF3B1 regardless of ring-sideroblast count and excludes SF3B1 VAF <10% (per Lee 2024; the ICC primary paper could not be read). _(evidence: [Khoury JD, et al. Leukemia. 2022;36(7):1703-1719](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9252913/); [Malcovati L, et al. Blood. 2020;136(2):157-170](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7362582/); [Lee WH, et al. Blood Cancer J. 2024;14(1):57](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11004131/))_

The CDC review gives 30–40% during early infection (standard two-tier table values for erythema migrans: acute phase 38–42%, convalescent phase 26–61%), and Branda & Steere 2021 cite 14% in solitary erythema migrans in one study; the figure depends on assay, stage and sampling time. _(evidence: [Moore A, et al. Emerg Infect Dis. 2016;22(7):1169-1177](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4918152/); [Branda JA, Steere AC. Clin Microbiol Rev. 2021;34(2):e00018-19](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7849240/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 61 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 presents EIA followed by IgM/IgG immunoblot as the standard algorithm without noting that since 29 July 2019 FDA-cleared modified two-tier assays (a second EIA in place of the immunoblot) are accepted by CDC as alternatives; the Number Vault mentions modified two-tier only in passing. _(evidence: [Mead P, Petersen J, Hinckley A. MMWR Morb Mortal Wkly Rep. 2019;68(32):703](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6818702/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 61 · High-Yield Hits #14 — Hit 14 labels Borrelia mayonii as upper Midwest. The original six patients were residents of Minnesota, North Dakota or Wisconsin (with tick-habitat exposure in Minnesota or Wisconsin), but a locally acquired human case with infected local Ixodes scapularis ticks was reported in New York in 2025. _(evidence: [Nafiz TN, et al. MMWR Morb Mortal Wkly Rep. 2026;75(21):271-272](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13235943/); [Pritt BS, et al. Lancet Infect Dis. 2016;16(5):556-564](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4975683/))_
- **needs-verification** · uncertain · Ch. 61 · High-Yield Hits #9 — Hit 9 states the tick must feed >24–48 h to transmit efficiently. A review of the transmission-time data argues that the minimum attachment time for transmission in humans has never been established, with experimental animal data showing transmission in <16 h and frequently <24 h (although risk rose with longer attachment); no guideline-level source was read to settle the figure. _(evidence: [Cook MJ. Int J Gen Med. 2015;8:1-8](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4278789/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 49 · High-Yield Hits #8 — Hit 8 calls natalizumab anti-alpha4/beta1. Natalizumab binds the alpha4 chain shared by alpha4beta1 and alpha4beta7, so it blocks both; its PML risk is attributed (hypothesised) to blocking alpha4beta1-VCAM-1 entry of immune cells into the CNS, while vedolizumab binds alpha4beta7 only. 'No PML in trials to date' for vedolizumab holds for the 2017-2018 reports read (Card 2018 estimates the risk as unlikely above 6.75 per 100,000 patient-years, not zero); later post-marketing data were not checked. _(evidence: [Luzentales-Simpson M, et al. Front Cell Dev Biol. 2021;9:612830](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7887288/); [Glenn T, et al. Front Neurol. 2025;16:1575653](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12226301/); [Card T, et al. Inflamm Bowel Dis. 2018;24(5):953-959](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6176885/))_
- **verified-issue** · missing-context · Ch. 49 · High-Yield Hits #11 — Hit 11 lists benralizumab among the anti-IL-5 drugs. Mepolizumab and reslizumab bind IL-5 itself, but benralizumab binds IL-5 receptor alpha and depletes eosinophils by ADCC. Dupilumab's approved sinus indication is chronic rhinosinusitis with nasal polyps rather than chronic sinusitis in general. _(evidence: [Gyawali B, Georas SN, Khurana S. Eur Respir Rev. 2025;34(175):240088](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11707604/))_

- Provenance: **reviewer-derived** — Derived from the user's reviewer, MEMORY LAB: The Memory-First Medical Laboratory Science Reviewer (A. Almoradie, 1st ed., v2.0, 2026; EPUB). Domains are the reviewer's 8 Parts, topics are its 79 chapters, and concepts are its High-Yield Hits, tables, figures, Exam Traps and special sections, located by chapter/section/item (the EPUB has no fixed page numbers). Legend: ✅ complete lesson · 🟡 complete lesson that still requires accuracy/expert review · 🟠 draft that fails the completeness check · ⬜ no lesson yet. Titles, placeholders and drafts are never counted as complete.

## Exam track outline verification

### MLS(ASCP) / MLS(ASCPi) certification

- ⚠️ UNVERIFIED — [MLS(ASCP) Examination Content Guideline (official PDF)](https://www.ascp.org/content/docs/default-source/boc-pdfs/boc-us-guidelines/mls_content_guideline.pdf) (2026-10-07): The official PDF returned HTTP 403/404 from the build environment on 2026-10-07, so content areas and weights below come from secondary summaries and are UNVERIFIED. Two secondary summaries disagreed on Microbiology (17–20% vs 17–22%). Download the current guideline from ascp.org and update this file.
- ⚠️ UNVERIFIED — [Reviewer Appendix A — ASCP BOC exam-blueprint coverage map](#/coverage) (2026-10-07): The reviewer states Blood Banking, Chemistry, Hematology and Microbiology at 17–22% each and Urinalysis/Body Fluids, Immunology and Laboratory Operations at 5–10% each, and treats molecular chapters (67–79) as cross-cutting. This is the reviewer's statement, not the official guideline; still awaiting the official ASCP PDF.
- ⚠️ UNVERIFIED — [MLS(ASCPi) international guideline](https://www.ascp.org/content/board-of-certification/international-certification) (2026-10-07): Not checked. ASCPi has its own content guideline; confirm whether its areas and weights differ from the US MLS guideline before relying on this track for ASCPi.

| Area | Weight | Weight verified |
|---|---|---|
| Blood Banking | 17–22% per reviewer Appendix A (unverified; one secondary source said 17–20%) | NO |
| Urinalysis and Other Body Fluids | 5–10% (reviewer Appendix A and secondary sources agree; unverified) | NO |
| Chemistry | 17–22% per reviewer Appendix A (unverified; one secondary source said 17–20%) | NO |
| Hematology | 17–22% per reviewer Appendix A (unverified; one secondary source said 17–20%) | NO |
| Immunology | 5–10% (reviewer Appendix A and secondary sources agree; unverified) | NO |
| Microbiology | 17–22% per reviewer Appendix A (unverified; one secondary source said 17–20%) | NO |
| Laboratory Operations | 5–10% (reviewer Appendix A and secondary sources agree; unverified) | NO |

### Philippine Medical Technologist Licensure Examination (MTLE)

- ✅ verified — [R.A. No. 5527 (Philippine Medical Technology Act of 1969), Sec. 17 — Scope of Examination](https://lawphil.net/statutes/repacts/ra1969/ra_5527_1969.html) (2026-10-07): Read 2026-10-07. Sec. 17 lists subjects and relative weights (below) and allows the Board to change subjects or weights with approval.
- ✅ verified — [PRB of Medical Technology Resolution No. 13, s. 2023 — Enhanced Tables of Specifications](https://www.prc.gov.ph/sites/default/files/2023-13%20published_medtech.pdf) (2026-10-07): Resolution text read 2026-10-07: adopts the enhanced TOS (Annex A) from the August 2023 MTLE onward and supersedes Res. No. 12 s. 2009. Annex A itself (topics, item counts, current weights) was NOT retrieved — prc.gov.ph returned HTTP 403. Topic lists in this app are therefore not TOS-verified.

| Area | Weight | Weight verified |
|---|---|---|
| Clinical Chemistry | 20% | yes |
| Microbiology and Parasitology | 20% | yes |
| Hematology | 20% | yes |
| Blood Banking and Serology | 20% | yes |
| Clinical Microscopy (Urinalysis and other body fluids) | 10% | yes |
| Histopathologic Technique (current scope per secondary sources also includes cytology, MT laws, laboratory management and ethics) | 10% | yes |
